PET 1308

PUPPY & 'MIDGET BISCUITS The Old and Reliable Foods.

AGENTS:

CARTER & SON Ltd. BRIDGE ST., WARRINGTON.

REARING CHICKENS NATURALLY. The time of year has npw arrived when broody hens are plentiful—too plentiful to suit some people's taste—and in the course of a week or two rearing in the natural manner will be in full swing. So far as feeding and general treat- ment of chickens reared naturally and artifi- cially are concerned, there is not a great deal of difference,_ but.in the housing there are, of course, many points of dissimilaritt. Early in the season a double coop is recommended, for the single form affords too little protiktion from wind and rain. Attached to the coop proper, Avhich should measure about 21in. square, there should be a covered run, measuring 21in. by 2ft. 3in., making a total for the double coop of ift. 9in. by 4ft. The inner compartinent should have the back end, front and top solid, but the end adjoining the run should consist of bars of wood placed about 2in. apart, so that the chic- kens can pass to and fro between the!, coop proper and the run, while the hen is ,confined within. There is no need to feed the youngsters during the first day or two after their exit from the shell, since what remains of the yolk sac is absorbed into the body of the chicken imme- diately previous to hatching, and this contains .Sufficient nutriment to sustain it for the period named. During the first few hours of the hen's occupancy of the coop she should be examined, for some hens are so overjoyed at regaining their comparative freedota that they are liable to forget their duties.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
FATTENING CHICKENS. All the available chickens should be fattened and marketed during April and May, and Pre- paration should now be made for preparing them in a proper manner. A fact to which have drawn attention before in these columns' is that it pays infinitely better to send a chicken to market when it weighs only 211 b. or 31b., but is worth at the very least is. 3d. per lb., than to wait a few weeks longer and market the same bird when it weighs, say, in., but only fetches 9d. or 10d. per lb. Apart from the fact that more money is actually secured for the smaller specimen, there is a very considerable saving in food and labour. Those who are for- tunate 'enough to possess some forward birds should make the very most of their opportunity. For two or three weeks before killing the chickens should be confined in a_ coop or small shed, and allowed as little exercise as possible, in order to encourage the production of flesh. They should be fed three times a day on mash and not on grain, as the former is so much more easily assimilated. Ground oats moistened with skim milk is the best food, providing flesh of first-class texture and colour.—" Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
ROSECOMB BLACK LEGHORNS.

The Rosecomb Black Leghorn is fast becom- ing very popular, and deservedly .so, as the comb not only gives the birds ra, very handsome apperance, but also in the cocks does away witrthe necessity of dubbing for stock, and in hens is no impediment to sight in feeding, as the large single-combs are. The` smaller area and thicker substance of comb exposed to the atmosphere cause frost-bite to be unknown, and as a consequence in cold weather cocks remain vigorous, and hens continte to lay unchecked. As producers of good-sized eggs (many laid in winter) Rosecombs are- unsurpassed, if not un- equalled. Their eggs have a high percentage of fertility, and chicks are. hardy and easy to rear, so that at an early age the best specimens can be selected, and the surplus stock is of a marketable size and quEility; and by no means to be despised as a table -delicacy. The Rose- comb Black Leghorn has been called the rich man's 'pride and the poor man's egg-producer„ It is one of the few varieties that will actually pay its own way, and is the variety for cold and exposed situations. Its fine stamina, high utility qualities, and handsome appearance will ensure- it a future of well-deserved popularity.

RUNAWAY HUSBANDS.

Insurance Cards As a Means Of Tracing Them.

GUARDIANS' SUGGESTION.

At the meeting of the, Runcorn Board of Guardians on Tuesday., a letter was read from the Merthyr Tydfil Union enclosing a. resolu- tion to, which they asked the suPport of the Guardians. The resolution ran as follows : "That this Board appears hi the Local' Gov- ernment Board to ask the National Health Assurance Commissioners to assist Poor Law Authorities in tracing, through the medium of insurance cards, men ,who have deserted their wives and fanrilies, thus causing them to become Chargeable to such authorities." Mr. Morris observed' that the resolution was a move in the right direction and thought they ought to support it. He moved that they do . •fr . so.

Mr. Woodward seconded. , He said that:any- , thing which would facilitate the discovery of people who deserted their wives and families should have the support of the Board. The Chairman said that in his opinion there was no, reason why the resolution should not be supported. They -needed gimething,Ao• faci- litate them in finding the whereabouts of wife- deserters. . _ • . -

The , Clerk observed that'the. suggested scrutiny 'would be very useful.. . . . . . The propqsal to support the resolution was carried unailiiriously. .

PEARSON AND KNOWLES.

Outlook Satisfactory, But Care

Needed.

Mr. A. Brotherton, secretary of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Ltd., in the half-yearly statement to shareholders which usually announces the amount of interim dividend, says :

"While the general. outlook is satisfactory and: the demand for the company's productions is maintained—in ' view of the abnormal conditions arising from the war and particu- larly ;having regard to uncertainties connected with the supplies of labour and ematerial, the directors have decided not to declare ah interim divislend upon the ordinary shares, but to post-1;01'W the., consideration of the question until -finally dealt with at the end of the company's financial year. "The dividends on the preferehce and second preference shares will be paid as usual on March 29th."

Acids should be avoided when cleaning zinc Utensils, but paraffin is excellent for the pur- pose, giving a clean, bright surface if finished off with a little dry whiting. Use a nice soft piece of chamois litither for the purpose.

By cleaning frying-pans with a little- salt im- mediately after use it keeps them in good condi- tion and prevents 'contamination and burning of other foods.

Rusty irons should be cleaned by rubng them with beeswax and lard, but, before using them, pass them .quickly over a sheet of brown paper that has ben sprinkled with dry salt. The nuisance of creaky hinges may be got over by rubbing a little mutton tallow in the joints or by working in a little powdereckblack- lead. This latter is a good remedy for locks or latches that go hard.

Rice Fritters (for persons). Wash a teacupful of rice;, pour over a pint of boiling water, and put in a jar in the oven, adding more Water until the rice is thoroughly soft and will not 'take up. more. All the water must be absorbed, otherwise the fritters will break in frying. Stir, four eggs well' into the warm rice. They bind the rice better if not beaten before being added. Season to taste. Melt some butter in 'a frying-pan and drop in a tablespoonful to each fritter, keeping them separate. These quantities will make a large dishful. - •

Cheese Dumplings (for four persons). Grate six ounces 'cheese pinto small pieces; chop an onion fine, add-. seasoning, and Mix. Have ready some paste; pull the mixture in the centre. Enclose and form into a ball; dust with flour, tie up in a cloth, and boil one-and-a-half hours.

Brown Gravy. Melt an ounce of 'butter in a saucepan, stir into it. a dessertspoonful of flour until the mix- ture browns. Add seasoning, pour on boiling water Or potato .water until the proper thick- ness i-s obtained; boil a minute or two and it is ready.

By MARJORIE.

Medical Women and their Valuable Work: War has given a remarkable impetus to the employment a women in the 'medical Profes- sion. So many practitioners are serving with the Army that county boards, county councils and committees are seeking in vain for doctors to fill ,the vacant places. Medical women are at a premium. To prepare for the career of a doctor is a somewhat costly affair, and it has been suggested that help should be given to- wards the training vf suitable women, whose limited circumstance A are a bar to their enter- ing the profession. The work of women in the war has been eulogised by Surgeon-General Sir Alfred Keogh, Director-General of the Army. He paid a warm tribute to the efficiency of their work, as well as to the unostentatious way in which it has been done. Sir Alfred is now engaged in completing a contract for 'a new military hospital, for 500 to 1,000 wounded sol- diers, and its medical staff will be composed entirely of women doctors.

The Volunteer Reserve Corps. As soon as the weather is sufficiently spring-

like, the Women's Volunteer Reserve Corps are going into camp. in different parts of England. fTwo large parks have been given for the pur- pose, one of which is situated near Woking. It appears that life under canvas is necestary before the women _can .offer their services to the War Office. There are many provincial branches of the Hon. Mrs. Haver&ld's Corps, and the countryside is accustoming itself to the unwonted spectacle—never before seen in England—of women in khaki unifornig, tramping the country roads like soldiers. The camps will be in every respect similar to those

HOME NOTES,

Profitable Recipes and Useful

Suggestions.

Brown Gravy, No. 2.

Cut up finely an onion, bruise a tomato, dredge or sprinkle over a tablespoonful of flour, and fry until nicely browned in about two ounces butter. Strain if desired, add boiling water until the desired thickness is obtained, add seasoning, boil a minute and it is ready. Blackberry Sponge.

- _ Fill an earthen basin closely with small cubes of bread, pouring over the bread, as it is fitted into place, hot blackberry, juice—blackberries cooked until soft with sugai to taste, and passed through a sieve. Use all the juice the bread will abSorb. Set the sponge aside in a cool place for some hours, then turn from the mould. Serve with blackberries and cream.

The Mayor of one East Coast town will, in case of invasion, call out the Women's Volunteer Reserves, in his district, anci thieir. duty' will. be the care of the sick, the aged, and the children in certain defined areas. The Corps will shortly have a 'flying contingent attached to it. An Anti-Cat Crusade.

crusasle tlifit would meet with little ap- proval in this country hits been started in the United States of America, by the members of the Burrough's Nature Club. It has been estimated that , there are in Brooklyn alone nearly one hundred and seventy thousand cats, and as each feline is an enemy to bird life, the members of this Nature Club have decided that a large proportion of the total is quite unneces- sary. Their immediate consignment to the lethal chamber is therefore advocated. As many cat Owners may object to the wholesale destruction of their pets, the members suggest that the law should step in and regulate the number of cats in proportion to the inhabitants of each city and town. Cats have been known to convey infection to human beings, from house to house, and their destruction is also advised on hygienic grounds.

The Popularity of Covert Coating. The tailor-made will reign supreme in Lon- don this spring, and a material that will rival serge is covert coating. The best makes come from the West of England, and are so light that the wearer hardly notices the extra fulness in the wider skirts. &These stitings are now made in many shades of biscuit, fawn, green, and grey. Grey is especially in demand, par- ticularly for sports costumes. A characteristic of covert coating is its neatness and trimness when made up, and its well-wearing qualities are another recommendation. The pleated skirt or basque in this material is in every way a success, and three-quarter coats for wear in the changeable days of early spring are at their best when built of covert coating. The ne ay. est shapes often. have 'the fulness from the shoulder, both back and front, but it is so cleverly arranged as to, appear to start from

the rather low waistline, which is confined with a belt, sometimes finished with a. buckle.

Buckles and Crystal Ornaments. Buckles of every conceivable kind are worn on millinery, coats, dresses and shoes. For the latter, coloured paste, and even pearLs, are used, in designs of many kinds. The big silver buckles of the eighteenth century have been revived for evening wear, and in cut steel, jet, and oxydised metal, there are numerous new designs. Buttons are also made in all these materials, as well as in crystal and nickel. Rock crystal is having quite a vogue, or rather has had for some time, and there is every prob- ability that it will last throughout the summer. The stones are not costly, yet they are very beautiful and• very suitable for present wear, since elaborate and ostentatious jewellery is out of place. These crystals are set in silver, or in platinum, and good paste, and pearls are often used with them. They are also some- times set in such a way that they suggest z •con- I stones or star sapphires. The new pendants in rock crystals are really beautiful ornaments. Straight bead chains with tasselled ends to harmonise in colour with blouses or gowns, are also having a vogue.

Rice Mould Custard.

Wash one-half cup of rice and cook until per- fectly tender. When done, mould in cups, fill- ing about half-full, and serve with a boiled custard made of one pint of milk, three table-

spoonfuls of sugar, a teaspoonful of cornflour and one egg.

England Responsible for Pie.

Though the people of Europe—and the British among them—regard pie .as an American insti- tution,. and while one Paris cafe goes to the trouble of printing "Pie Americaine " on its mopu, the truth is that England is responsible for all the indigestion the pastry causes. Pie came to the fore in England many cen- turies ago. It originated in the form of mince pie and was used in the' celebration of Christ- mas. In its primitive stage is was baked in a deep-sided dish, lined and covered with rolled- out dough. The filling was of force meats, richly seasoned and spiced. This spicing and flavouring stood for the presents which the Wise Men bore to tile Christ in the manger.

For years and years this custom of having the Christmas mince pie prevailed, but finally was denounced far and wide by the Puritans as a form of idolatry and the Government after Parliament had suppressed the celebration of the birth of -Christ took steps to stop the baking and eating of the taince pie. Eventually saner reasoning led to the taking off of the ban and the pie-eating custom was renewed. .:t..Ajaaericans hold that the development of the fiiik art of pie-making was,, left to .America. Pumpkin pie is an American, creation, as are several other varieties.

"A BIRO IN THE HAND.°

It was mdlket, day in Wilverton, and all the farmers from the country round were coming into town to. buy and sell. Mr. Greggs was walking very fast to catch a train, which would take him inip-Vilvertim, when he overtook Mr. Johnson, who,capried a: bag and Was also walking very fast. "Good-day, Johnson," said Mr. Greggs. "God-day," ,replied Mr. Johnson; "going for the train, eh ?'7 .

"Yes," said , Mr, Greggs. "Pretty sharp work, I fancy. -It's two minutes to .nine by the church clock, and the train gees at seven min- utes past." Mr. Johnson said, "I have sent my pigs round by road to Wilverton, and they will be there before me if I miss the train."

•".And I 'am after a good cow," said Mr. Grego. If I miss the train all the best ones will be bought up 'beforeq get to market." . "I, wish a cart would come along and give us a lift," .sighed Mr. Johnson. "So- do L". answered the otter. "Why, here comes Hardy 'with his 'donkey cart. I have a good,,mind to ask him to tOce me in." •••'

"How Mulish," grumbleCi Mr, Johnson, "You would get to tho• station as soon walking. Pin going on•tig I see a good: trotter. coming along."

"Right you are," replied Mr. Greggs cheerily. "I have heard say that a bird in the hand is worth two .in the ,bush, and I reckon a real donkey coming along the road is worth a fast- trotting horse in your imagination. Good-day, Hardy ! Are you for the station?" he called out. ,

"Yes, come along," exclaimed Hardy, pulling up. Mr. Greggs climbed into the little cart,. and the donkey trotted gaily along—not very fast, it must be confessed, but, still, faster than Greggs could walk. They reached the station just as the train ar- rived, and Mr. Greggs jumped out of the Cart. After thanking the kind driver, .he :just had time to buy. his railway ticket . and enter the train before it started.

• • M they steamed out on the way to Wilvertmi, poor',Vr. lohasoft same panting up the road— very -hot- kt:9lldusty, He had evidently met no fast-trotting hot And cart. "Hallo!" cried Mr. kregga, out of the, train window; "you see, a bird iil the hand was worth two in the bush !"

A FISHING LESSON. • (By A1.1,1N CAMERON.) Three little' men ii big straw -hats • Went out to fish for tittlebats.. The first one said: "I know the way; I'm sure I'll catch most fish, to-day." The second said: "I. think you'll see The biggest lot will come to me." The third wee mail said: "Time will tell." And now I'll. show you what befell.

The first broUght home an empty dith— Hq Caught- a single -fish The second had a better fate; Before he stopped he'd captured eight. The third, YOu know, .had made no boast, Yet (strange to say) he got the most. So, if you wish to have success, I think the moral you can guess.

Beaded Blouses.

A very pretty idea is the blouse for indoor wear that is trimmed with beadwork. It is made of any filmy material, of crepe de chine, or charmeuse, if preferred. Indoor blouses this spring are elaborate garments, possibly because those for wearing with coats and skirts are severity itself. The beads used for these blouses are small and iridescent, and some have the effect of moonlight, others are of the most deli- cate tints of rose, blue; green and orange, and when tastefully blended, the effect is charm- ing. Patterns of tiny flowers may be worked over the, bodice at intervals, or a high collar and cuffs only are worked. Sometimes flowers are embroidered in outline with oloitred filo- selles, and then iridescent -beads are car?:!.-t=3lv strewn over them, or the edges of the flowers are outlined and the centres filled with these tiny pearls, as the French call them. When roses are chosen the foliage should be of green beads in various shades. Clear glass beads give the effect of, dewdrops, and lilies of the valley afford admirable effects. The work is quickly done, is very fascinating, and the °materials for it are by no means costly, but it is expensive to buy ready-made.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information in Popular Language.

Dangerous Sweets. At Barrow Police Court, two Italian confec- tioners were summoned for selling sweets known as "chocolate humbugs," which the Public Analyst certified to contain a proportion of oleo resin of capsicum, equal to twelve minims of tincture of capsicum of the British Pharma- copceia strength. The Medical Officer of Health for the borough said that each chocolate con- taine,d sufficient capsicum to cause acute gas- tritis. In the case of a weak-chested person hemorrhage of the lungs might be caused, and a child might have spasms which would prove fatal. •

• The High Tea Delusion. The Council schools are educating girls, and in some few instances boys, in cooker, and pos- sibly the world may benefit by this. But is any attempt made to inform these young people of the effect of -certain . combination7-7.1.‘p0n. - the human system? And how much of this very necessary knowledge is in the possession of even the highly paid cooks employed by the well-to- do? Many thousands of English families par- take habitually of that really awful meal, high tea: It is an especial favourite in the country, where it serves the purpose of tea and supper combined to the exclusion of dinner. Anywhere between 6 o'clock and 8 this terrible repast is served, and anyone possessing imagination can 'picture the family party assembled together and eating- to the ruin of their digestive organs. It 'is a fearsome thing to see them swallow boil- ing tea after slices of ;cold beef or cold 'pork, 'accompanied most probably, to make the, matter worse, by pickles. The tea (or coffee) turns the meat into a substance resembling leather. (What it makes of pickles must be left to the imagina- tion The palate ought to be a protection I • against such horrors. Nature gives most of us a sense of taste more or less keen, but we play such tricks with it, that it soon becomes com- paratively useless. The palate is the door porter .of the establishment, and should not admit any- thing doubtful to the internal economy. Among 'things that spbil the palate are excessive smok- ing, indulgence in strong drinks, and dishes that are very highly seasoned.—" Gentlewoman." Damp Beds. , A damp bed is even more' dangerous than damp sheets. If there is any doubt about the dryness it is better to sit up. all night than. sleep in a damp 'bed. The temperature of the whole system becomes so much reduced during sleep that it cannot ward off the dangers of chill in the, same way as when. awake, and the body is more open to malign influences when at rest than at any other time. Many diseases, fatal in their effect, have been set up by sleep- ing in a damp bed. I have even known loss of sight in a. young girl traced Ito sleeping. in a damp bed when staying with some friends. It is stated that a good test to discover whether the bed is damp is to place the looking-glass between the-sheets, and leave, it there for some minutes. If the bed is dainp the glass will be clouded and dull when removed from the bed.— M.G. in "Farm and Home."

FARM AND GARDEN.

A BIG CHEESE.

The town of Cheshire, Mass., was settled by dairy farmers from Cheshire, England, and for years its cheese was the most noted in this country. When Thomas Jefferson was inaugu- rated 'President the farmers of the town con-

tributed one day's milk from their ,dairies and had it made into an enormous cheese weighing 1,600 pounds and sent it to the President at Baltimore.

SALE OF ARMY MARES FOR BREEDING. With a view of encouFaging and assisting the breeding of light horses': the Board of Agricul- ture and Fisheries have been authorised by the War Office to arrange for the sale of some mares which have been returned from abroad as no longer suitable for use with the Expeditionary Force, and which hate been specially selected by the Board as types suitable for breeding pur- poses. The mares will be kept under the care and observation of the Board for a month after their return from abroad, and will then -be sold by public auction on , the express condition that ,they are not at any time to be exported. To secure observance of this condition the mares will be branded with a distinctive mark of dia- mond shape. The mares have passed the mallein test for glanders to the satisfaction of the Board's vet- erinary officers, but no guarantee is given as to their age, soundness or otherwise. The mares can be examined prior to disposal at the places of sale by veterinary surgeons on behalf of ,in- tending purchasers. The mares can be seen at the Cattle Testing Station, Pirbright, surrey (Woking Station five miles; Brookwood Station three miles) by ar- rangement with the Inspector-in-Charge. It is hoped to arrange for sales in various parts of the country during the next few months The Board take this opportunity of drawing the attention of owners of mares to the fact that

all premium stallions subsidised by the Board will travel during the forthcoming season at the low service fee of El.

ALLEGED HOLDING-UP OF WHEAT.

At the last Council meeting of the Central and Associated Chambers of Agriculture, the fol- lowing resolution was unanimously dopted : "That this Council desires to repudiate the

allegation which has appeared in some organs of the Press that farmers as a class are holding up their supplies of wheat to the detriment of national interests."

MILK AND CORN

As the end of March approaches milk pro- ducers will find it essential to decide what they will do with regard to their contracts for future supplies to the trade. As I write the prices at the great London stations vary from 14d. to 161 d. per imperial gallon;. at Birmingham they. were lid; to 15d.; and at Manchester 14d. to 19d. on different days of the ,past week. These figures are beyond anything I remember in a long experience, and fully warrant the increase which has been made in some towns to 44d. ,a 'quart. There is, hOwever; no reason to suppose that milk will be so scared by the end of the month, when cows .will have calye4, and .wbeTl 'more• -genial weather may be expected. 'Fit.r- mers have had to contend against terrible x9a- ther. It is well to remember that cattle are dear, and that in consequence more heifer calves should be reared. If I were still farming and a producer of milk, I should be tempted to ignore contracts and take my chance. There need be no loss; for butter is certain to sell well, and cheese into the bargain, although this is much harder to make without equipment or training. Most readers who are interested in corn grow- ing know very well that our imports have I jarg,ely- decreased from some countries, or ceased altogether, and that there is no reason to sup- pose the position will be improved in the winter to come. Oats came to us chiefly from Argen- tina, Germany, Russia, the United States, Chili, and Roumania, in 1913 in the circler named, while from BritiSh pOssessions we obtained large con- signments from Canada. I have Ro doubt that the Dominion will do better than ever before, but as we are certain to lose the German con- signment, which reached nearly 3i million ewt., ‘and a large portion of: that from Russia, apart from smaller lots from Turkey and Roumania: it is obvious that oats will still pay to grow. Unlike barley, the oat is a popular foodstuff, and it will be much wiser to grow. it 'instead, and I appeal as much to the profit side of the question as to the patriotic side. • It is quite on the cards that beer will be much less consumed. for some time at least. To grow barley as usual will therefore be a risky undertaking. If a good strong cropper is selected, oats will pay well if anured with phosphates and nitrogen.—Pro- lessor Long in "Farm and Home."

PROFIT IN HONEY.

At a time like the present, when those who are unable to assist their country in the most prac- tical manner of all, are urged insistently to "do their' bit," surely we might pay more attention to that most neglected oi crafts, apiculture. I read in the "Bee Journal" recently that the value of honey imported into the United King- dom for last year was £37,662. The figures speak for themselves, answering plainly thei question whether honey is a popular food or not. Why do not we try to secure some of this valu- able trade? Of course, in recent years that pre- valent scourge, Isle of Wight disease, has been disheartening, carrying off whole colonies; but (and one can only . hope that Nemesis is not listening round the corner!) its inroads seem a trifle less virulent now, and we may venture to hope that, as in the case of a human epidemic, the disease has begun to spend its force.—" Agr- icultural Economist and Horticultural Reviewe:

SOOT-WATER FOR PLANTS.

Soot-water, when apnli.-.41 to rlauts, slinul4l be nuit,- clear and free froui all sefliment; other- wise the soil on the top will lu ye a dirty; black look. We-have lately been using it very eak some aspidistras, some iVaves of. which vs.:limed a yellow, unhea!tby appearance, and the leaves have now a liars green hue. 'These plants have not been re-pottA for two lea and the pats are quite full of roots. Being large plants, and not wishing t..) incr3ase the size the pots, we have found this watering with soot- wat,tr of great benefit. The best way of mak- ing soot-water is to place a peck of soot in a canvas bag into a tub holding from 30 gallons -to 40 gallons of water, and then add half a pint of this soot-water to every gallon of clear water that-is used for the plants. Many plants will take it much stronger than this, but it is always wise to be on the safe side, and use it more frequently in a weaker solution.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
ROSE PRUNING

Many amateurs fall into the too common error of pruning their roses too soon. When: they see the rose bushes pushing out green leaves at the tips pf last year's wood they fancy they are all behind if they do not start prun- ing. The experienced grower, however, waits until 'March is well advanced before he cuts back. to the buds he hopes to get his best flow- ers from. Then as we usually get cold winds and frosts at night it is some time before these firm buds' start sufficiently to take any harm. If we leave the pruning tilt March is well ad- vanced these unseasonably made growths can then be cut away,' leaving the lower buds tightly folded up, these When more genial wea- ther comes in April opening freely. an,. addi- tion to pruning too soon, the majority of ama- teurs do not prune hard enough, as they leave too much small weakly wood that Ails, up the centre of the bush. Far better cut away all weakly wood and shorten the strong shoots to a good bud, which in time, if all goes well, will result in a handsome bloom.—" Farm and Home."

FELL IN THE FIRE.

Earlestown Youth's Awful Fate

Mr. Coroner Brighouse conducted an inquiry at the Warrington Coroner's Court on Satur- day afternoon into the circumstances attend- ing the death of George Chadwick, aged 15, who died in the Warrington Infirmary on the pre- vious Thursday from burns received on Febru- ary 17th.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
FAINTED AND FELL INTO THE FIRE.

Frances Chadwick, of 44, Gas-street, Newton- le-Willows, sister of the deceased, deposed that at 9 a.m. on the morning of February 17th she called her brother down to his breakfast, which she had left prepared for him on the kitchen table. She went out to post a leder and on-re- turning at 9.30 found a neighbour, Mr. Hazel- ton, in the house, who told her that her brother had been badly burned. Dr. Elliott attended and ordered her brother's removal to the War- rington Infirmary. Her brother got up to reach the teapot and while doing so fainted and fell into the fire. She had visited him at the In- firmary and was told his injuries were serious. On March 4th she was informed that her brother was worse, and she went to the Infirm- ary and stayed there until death took place at 1.20 on Thursday afternoon. He had been sub- ject to fits all his life and did no work. He had a fit a week prior to the accident,

On February 17 she heard someone maning and on going into Chadwick's house found the boy lying unconscious on the floor, his ..5---71; on fire. She extinguished the flames and got assistance. 'A verdict of "Death from accidentally burn- ing" was returned. -- •

RED CROSS MEN

Receive Instruction and Certificates from Dr. Ogilvie.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
"A RATTLING GOOD LOT."

The presentation of first-aid certificates to successful members of the men's detachment of the Latchford Division of the British Red Cross Society formed a happy little function at the Raddon Court Red Cross Hospital on Saturday afternoon. The preftentations were made by Dr. lan Ogilvie (class lecturer), and Miss Sylvia Broadbent (divisional command- ant) occupied the chair. Mrs. Wheeler (divi- sional secretary) and Miss Appleton were among those present.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
DETACHMENT INCOMPLETE.

Miss Sylvia Broadbent welcomed Dr. Ogilvie, and assured him of their deep gratitude' for his two courses of lectures to the men's class, which had done extremely well. The doctor had very kindly promised to become medical officer to the men's detachment. (Applause.) As' yet they would not be able to form a complete detachment. It had been hoped that they would be able to co-operate with the Daresbury men to form a complete detachment from the two districts; but unfortunateiy the distance prevented that—though they might hold competitions. She trusted the township- leaders would get further members. Op- Plause.) • Dr. Ogilvie said it had been a great pleasure to him to conduct the lectures; and in spite of the difficulties the class had had, he was pleased to say that Dr. G. W. N. Joseph, after the examination, said they were 'a rattling g.ood lot of candidates." He put that .own to the men's own efforts; he was sure they de- served all the marks they got. (Amuse, THE RECIPIENTS.

Mrs. Wheeler handing., them. to him, Dr. Ogilvie then: presented an efficiency medal to Mr. Richards and certificates to Messrs. L. Appleton (advanced) T. Blease, W. Darbyshire, A. J. Darlington, Gilbert Ford, Charles Foster, Matthew S. Gaulter, Fred Longshaw, Arthur Massey; A. Butter, J. Smith, T. Starkey and Thomas Taylor, and Private S. Pyne. Mrs. Wheeler proposed , a hearty vote of thanks to Dr. Ogilvie, and also to Dr. Joseph for conducting the examination. She did not know bow they had managed to spare the time-.'-especially at present. Even in times of peace doctors seemed t ia,ve no time to spare. The motion was cai MO' with applause. On behalf of the men's class, Mr. Fred Long- shaw (lecture-secretary) presented Dr. Ogilvie with a handsome silver-mounted umbrella, and Mr. William A. Banning—who, he said, had "helped them very much before they had the doctor"--with a silver-mounted walking-Stick. The recipients replied in terms of, delitht. MEN'S ARDUOUS WORK.

Miss Sylvia Broadbent proposed a vote of thanks to the men's detachment for their arduous work at Raddon Court. If the un- expected happened, they would find themselves much better able to serve amongst the wounded. She hoped all' who could would fol- low the example of Private Pyne. She was extremely grateful to the Secretary (Mr. Long- shaw). 'Sister Ellis seconded on behalf of the hospi- tal matron and staff. As she lived there, she said, she knew the good work the men's detach- ment had done. The motion was carried unanimously, and Mr. Longshaw briefly replied. The men would try, he said, to get everythiug into the best Possible working order.

-Ungland !" They had already seen that Eng- land, by standing in the way, had wrested from them the success which, in their own minds, they had planned out as an absolute certainty.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
The Situation Looking Brighter.

" The sky," said Mr. Rylands, in conclusion, • is looking rather brighter. We have checked the rush, and seeing that we have done that and 'that Germany was prepared, whilst we were un- Prepared, I think we may, with some confidence, lee' that the boot is rather on the other leg and that we are now getting prepared, whilst they are getting exhausted. The tide is beginning to move, and to move strongly, in the right direction, and it will roll on and on until Ger- taany will be compelled to crave for peace; a Peace which, we trust, may be on such terms as Will draw tlu3 teeth of Germany and will take away from the world a blot, a constant danger, a constant scourge." (Applause.) • Mr. G. H. Smith seconded the proposition, Which was carried with acclamation. AND SO SAY ALL OF US. -

In reply Sir Gilbert Greenall said that he hoped that long before he was called upon to take the chair at the next yearly meeting of the Association the war would have been brought to, a successful end.

POLITICS PUT ASIDE.

Only One Object—To Trounce the

Germans.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSERVATIVE ANNUAL MEETING.

The whole of the country is proud of the Oppo- sition at the present moment, and I think the manner in which they have supported the Government during these last few trying months—an., I think, quite rightly supported them, too—speaks volumes for them. This war is something far above and beyond party politics, and we must put all that sort of thing aside just now, and one and all unite in doing our best to win in the greatest struggle the world has ever known.

These words occurred in the brief speech' with which Sir Gilbert Greenall opened the annual meeting of the. Warrington Conservative Asso- ciation, which was held in the hall of the Junior Conservative Club, Market-place, on Monday evening, and over which he presided, there being a gooct attendance of members. POLITICS BARRED; BUT STILL -

Earlier on in his speech:, Sir Gilbert said that politics Were barred, but:he did not think they should, for one moment, retrain from watching every point and eveiy movement in the political world, so that when the time came and the war was over they could, discuss them and criticise them without prejudice. They were all proud of what Warrington had done in connection with the war. The ranks of the Tory party were very much thinned owing to the number of men who had joined his Majesty's Forces. They hoped that those men would come safely back again and that when the war was over the Tory party in Warrington wonld be even stronger and better than it had been in the past, ready and willing to face the next election, come whenever it might. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150313_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
POLITICAL ACTIVITY SUSPENDED ON OUTBREAK OF WAR.

In the course of his annual report, the Secre- tary (Mr. G. H. Smith) said that during the first half of last year the activities of the various branches of the Conservative, organisation were well maintained, but, immediately after war was declared, all political work was suspended, so that their forces might be united with those of all parties in giving unflinching and whole- hearted support to the Government in the supreme task with which the country was so suddenly faced. This cessation of domestic strife was, in all probability, a factor not con- templated by Germany, and was not the least important of the many miscalculations made by the enemy in .her preparations for war.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
The Member on Active Service.

The report went on to refer to the marriage of the Member, .Mr. Harold Smith, and added that within a few days of the happy local pres- entation ceremonies, which marked the event, the war was thrust upon the country, and Mr. Smith hurried to town to take up duties as hon. secretary of the newly-formed Govern- ment Press Bureau, which office he held for several months, and which he only relinquished on receiving his commission as a hon. lieuten- ant in the Royal Naval Volunteer Reserve for Special Admiralty Service. In accordance with the political truce, the report continued, no opposition was offered by the Conservative and Liberal parties to the' return to the Town Council in November last of the retiring mem- bers, but contests were forced in three wards by Socialist candidates. Many Members With the Colours.

All three retiring councillors, however, easily held their seats. Sympathetic reference hav- ing been made to the deaths of the Right Hon. Joseph Chamberlain, Alderman Forshaw and Messrs. Jas'. Richardson, R. I. Wynne Jones, Austen Vernon, J. Grounds and Jas. Bell, the report went on to stat,e that.many of the mem- bers of the Association and Ward Committees had joined the colours, and that one member of the Council, Mr. Ralph Done, was serving with the Cheshire Regiment. "Ever- since the out- break of the war," the report concluded,. "Lady' Greenall and an influential committee and a splendid band of workers have rendered 'in- valuable service in supplying our soldiers and sail Ors with garments and a gigantic supply has been constantly maintained. Lady Greenall takes with her to the new duties she has so nobly undertaken at the Front, the sincerest wishes fot their successful accomplishment and for her safe return."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
FINANCES SATISFACTORY.

Mr. E. Bradshaw submitted his report as treasurer. The previous year, he said, the finances of the Association were satisfactory; last year they were more so. Although many calls were being made on their generosity, he thought they ought to remind themselves that the expenses of the Association were still run- ning on, and would .h.ave to be met. Although politics might• be banished for the moment, they should, nevertheless, keep their exchequer well replenished. ELECTION OF OFFICERS.

The secretary's -ii;ul the -ti.ea-sl-.,e;;s reports were adopted, on the proposition of Sir Gilbert Greenall, and the meeting proceeded with the election of officers for the ensuing year. On the proposition of Alderman Bennett, sec- onded by Mr. E. James Hall, Sir Gilbert Greenall was unanimously elected president. Messrs. Harold Smith, M.P. J. Charlton Parr and Robert Pierpoint were elected deputy- presidents, and Mr. W. Peter Rylands was again chosen chairman, with Mr. J. Fulton- Smith as deputy-chairman. Mr. G. H. Smith was re-elected hon. secretary aid Mr. Edwin Bradshaw treasurer. Mr. E. A. White was re-appointed hon. legal adviser.

GERMANY'S PREPARATIONS.

In moving a vote of thanks to Sir Gilbert Greenall,

"Anti yet," he said, "during all these years the Brunners and the Monds—and the Crosfields for all I recollect— have always maintained that the German peril was a bogey, that the Germans Joved us like brothers, and that the last nation in the world that they would ever attack was ourselves. It was difficult for us to .appreciate that attitude of mind; to realise how it could possibly be that a sensible man could suggest that the proper course, in face of what klermany was doing, was to scrap our Navy as a sign of our good inten- ttons towards the Fatherland, and as a sign of our perfect confidence that she 'would never hit us and never seek to pick a quarrel.

Political Truce, But Political Politeness The only explanation I. can come to is that there is something abOut the Radical mind that is very lacking in judgment. It is always dis- posed to believe what it wishes to believe.'! To fight this country was the last thing in the world that Germany. wished to do. Her idea bad been to isolate this nation from the other nations— to have her without a friend—and then gradu- ally, by material forces, backed by enormous physical forces, to wrest from her the premier position in the world. . BUSINESS "AS USUAL."

War Causes " Very Little Disturbance." In moving the adoption of the report . and balance sheet, the CHAIRMAN said that the year which had closed, though• favourable to the company, had been an anxious one for the directors, especially the latter. part of it. They began the year well and were going on, in every 'respect, as well as they could wish when war wars declared. The directors decided to carry on as usual, until it could be ascertained how the new situation would affect the com- pany, and -"they were gratified to find, after some weeks' close attention, that there was very little extra disturbance in the business of the company at the time than the cancel- lation.of a•few small ordert. The only element of, disturbaribe arose later on, .when the coun- try called for recruits for the Army, and a number. of the men employed by the company —about 300 up to date—left their employment to join the Army; a fact which, at the time, caused considerable inconvenience, it being difficult to secure others to replace them, with the result that the company's output of manul 'factured goods was reduced.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
A Weekly Allowance to Men at the Front.

A weekly sum was allowed to such of those men as had dependents, subject to, re-consid- eration by the directors. The new buildings at Grappenhall had made considerable pro- gress towards completion. It was found that more land would be required to complete the entire plan of the works than was at first expected, and de. plot of about six acres, adja- cent to the main works, being eligible and available, the directors took advantage of the opportunity to lease it. The directors believed that the complany was now in possession of all the land it might require in Grappenhall; at any rate, for many years to come. Raw mate- rial, so far as the company were concerned, had been in an unsteady state for some time, but they had watched the market pretty closely, and, on the whole, had purchased favourably. The position of coal at the present time was one for anxious thought. The price had risen so much that several Gas Companies had al- ready given notice of an increase in the price of gas, which might -possibly lead to a re- Stricted, use of some of the company's manu- factures. They dwould know more on that subject, howefer, that time next year, when the present year's accounts came up for con- sideration. The Chairman then proceeded to analyse the balance sheet and said that the ordinary liabilities were the same as last year.

On the Other side of the balance sheet the item in respect of freehold and leasehold, works, &c., showed a considerable addition to the assets, an addition, in fact, of £23,084 13s. 4d. They were unfortunate from the very start of the com- pany in not having the requisite buildings, and they had had to get them the best way they could from year to year, with the reiult that their premises were now getting into a large works. This year's addition brought the total value of the works, less depreciation, up to £132,499 10s. 34. The works were growing, and would have to grow more before they had fin- ished, because there were several departments that were not. properly housed yet, and the proper housing of them would' have to come.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COMPANY'S LATEST ACHIEVEMENT

A Gas Fire That Burns Noiselessly.

• Mr. H. :M. Tumarrox seconded the adoption of the report. The war, he said, had so altered ,the conditions of business in the coun- try that it was almost impossible to adequately convey to them what . the operations of the company during the year 1914 had been. They were fortunate, however, in that the business they were interested in Was one manufacturing almost everything in the nature. of gas appara- tus which could be considered really necessary fpr domestic and industrial use. The gas cooker, which had always been an important part of their business, had long ago estab- lished itself as an economical and labour-sav- ing appliance. The facilities offered for clean- liness and quickness in use, which they • had always made leading features in .their designs, had never been More valued and .14:Linable than during the last few months': when, unfortu- • mately, so many wounded and invalided men had returned to their homes and to the tem- porary hospitals. The gas fire, also, had become a necessary part of 'domestic equip- ment. During the last few- years their labora- tories had steadily app.lied .every- known science to the improvement of gas fires, and one of their latest achievements,was. a gas fire which was silent when burning. The importance of. this imprpvement, which they were the first to intilbduce and bring to a successful issue, had ,very greatly added to .the popularity of their gas fires, throughout the country. This feature of silent burning, was, considered such a decisive advance .that, the "Lancet "—recog- nised as the leading. medical jpirrnal—published an tirtiele fully describing the fires. Gas Stoves That Emit No .Fumes.

He need hardly point out that, gas fires were now strongly recommended by medical -men, and where they, were used for bedrooms—espe- cially in case of sickness—ie 'would be at once apparent why the introduction of silent gas fires had met with their further approval and support. Another feature of their gas fires was that, when fixed under usual and proper conditions, all products of. combustion, left the stove only by the flue outlet. The importance of this point could not be -too strongly repre- sented to the' public, now that ventilation and purity of atmosphere were So much discussed. The present extensive use of gas fires and all domestic gas apparatus, was, however, not due entirely to the improvements which he had mentioned, but to the consistent persistent, and convincing advertising of the British Com- mercial Gas Association. As a member of the Executive Committee, he was able to say. that that association had continued to honestly and carefully explain by every legitimate means the many advantages of the various types of apparatus at present available for use with gas. The gas industry, and they, as manufac- turers, recognised very fully the importance of supporting an association which appealed to the public so forcefully and successfully. It would be, gratifying to the shareholders to feel that they were interested in a company which had supplied, and was supplying, a very im- portant and almostPovrwhelming- demand for industrial gas appliances.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
New Trade Since the War Commenced.

Many of these were used in the preparation of armaments, transport vehicles, and, in fact, almost every part of ,the naval and military equipment. Thsir business in gas furnaces, which, for. several years, had been steadily growing, had, since the war commenced, been largely increased, and it was, quite clear to them that the outpht of many things required by their Imperial Forces had been speeded up and increased in quantity by the aid of Rich- mond's gas furnaces. (Applause.) One in- stance out of many which he could mention would give some idea of the ,national value of industrial gas: A well-known firm making musical instruments found the derhand for their manufactures almost stopped owing to the war. They had large premises and splen- did machinery. They immediately turned their attention to the possibility -of manufacturing ammunition, and; with the advice the Rich- mond Company were able to give the as to the use of gas furnaces and the installation of a Very big battery of these appliances, 'they were to-day one of the largest suppliers cf ammunition to the Government.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
Producing Many War-time Necessities._

It would also interest the shareholders to know that they had 'equipped a considerable number of temporary hospitals, barracks, &c., with .gas cooking and heating appliances; geysers, boilers, &c. From' what he had said it would be seen that this Company were actively assisting in the production of many things made necessary by the war—(applause) —and they hoped that bY so doing it was help- ing to bring nearer the day to which they were all looking forward when there .would he a .successful termination of hostilities. (Renewed applause.) In conclusion might he say how splendidly their officials, staff and workmen had, supported them during a vel.y difficult period, and, though they might have many unknown difficulties tti deal with in `;le. months before them, they felt satisfied that m ith their continued co-operation, added to the important position they had already attained in the gas world, he was satisfied that they 'might look forward with confidence to receiving a full share of whatever orders had to be placed by their many customers throughout the country. (Applause.) The •report was adopted.

Mr. riamblett drew attention to the matter of the education of children who were 'suffering from tuberculosis and plithisis. Ile was given to understand there were considerably over 300 children in Warrington suffering ,from the disease. The Education Authorities lost a sum of £2O per week through children being exempted from school through sickness. He suggested that a residential school ought to be provided. IN THE COURTS.

A WOULD-BE SUICIDE.

Depres,,ed Because He Has A Son At The War.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday, George Harry Howe, 107, Fox-street, a black- smith's striker, was charged with having at- tempted to commit suicide. on February 22nd. Mrs. Howe, wife of prisoner, spoke to having found her husband on the day in quiestion with his head' over a gas jet. He had been under the doctor for the past six months, and had complained of pains in the head. He had been in the Infirmary since February 22nd. He had a son at the war and this had depressed him.

Howe promised not to attempt anything of he sort again, and was discharged.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
TENDERED AN OLD TICKET.

Flow An Asylum Attendant Got Into Trouble.

At the Borqugh Police Court on Tuesday morning, before Councillor H. Roberts and Mr. C.. Jackson Holmes, .Tosepk Oakes, of 18, School-road, WinsfOrd, was summoned for hav- ing travelled on the London & North-Western Railway from Warrington to Over and Whar- ton without having previously iiaid his fare, and with intent to defraud.—He did not ap- pear, but was represented by Mr. J. Holland, solicitor, of Wirisford. Mr. Huddlestone, solicitor, who appeared for the railway company, said that when alighting at Over aud Wharton Station, after travelling up from Warrington, defendant gave up an out-of-date ticket issued for a journey between Warrington. and Moore. When questioned he said Milt he had lent his coat to a man, who must have left the out-of-date ticket to Moore in one of the, pockets, but, added Mr. Huddle- stone, he made no attempt to search his pockets and find the proper ticket, whilst he also refused tg discuss the matter further. Inquiry at Warrington showed that .no tickets had been issued for Over and Wharton Station for the particular train on which the defendant 'travelled. YIELDED TO SUDDEN TEMPTATION.

Mr. Holland told the Court that defendant was a married man and was an attendant at the Winwick County Asylum and only earned ..e.30 a year and his board. Apart from this pffence he had in absolutely clean record; in- deed, he was a man of exemplary character. Defendant had written instructing him to at- tend the Court and plead guilty for him. He had no time to book a ticket, owing to there being a crinh of soldiers round the booking office, and, in a moment of temptation, he very foolishly gave up the old ticket. He .had not gone to the station prepared to defraud the railway company; it was not a deliberate thing, but a sudden yielding to temptation. The Bench imposed a fine of 205., including costs. •

THURSDAY.

Before Messrs. A. J. Jolley (in the, chair), W. Roberts and W. T. Williams, and Dr. Naden. Theft of Flour.--Janies Hamblett, .5, Bond- street; John Williamson, 13, Bond-street; and Albert Gillimore, 41.; Hesketh-street, were in the dock together on a charge of having stolen a bag of flour, valued at .Cl, on the 9th inst., from a barge at Bishop's Wharf.—The three men, said 'the Chief Constable, we employed by the Monks, Hall Co. in loading and dis- charging cargoes. On Monday the men were engaged in unloading a barge containing steel billets. In the barge were four or fire bags of flour, which had not been delivered, because the bags had been damaged and if moved the flour would fall out. They had been placed in such a position as to be safe until the captain found an opportunity to unload them at Bir- kenhead. The discovery was made that -the flour had been removed by the three men and taken to their homes. Sergeant Reddington, accompanied by the Wharf policeman, made a search ar the prisoners' homes. They -found some flour in the -bedroom of one of the Men. In the kitchen of another of the trio some of the flour was on the .dresser, and in the case of the third man it was found concealed in a neighbour's house.—All three men pleaded guilty.—The manager of Messrs. Monks, Hall Co. gave the • men good characters, and the Chief Constable said there was nothing pre-. viously against them.—The men were fined stl each, and 7s. 6d. costs.

PRUDENTIAL ASSURANCE COMPANY, LIMITED.

Chief Office: HOLBORN BARS, LONDON.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Summary of the Report presented at the Sixty-sixth Annual Meeting, held on th March, 1915.

ORDINARY 'BRANCH.—The number of policies issued during the year was 65,751, assuring the sum of £6,318,843, and producing a new annual premium income of £424,353. The premiums received dur- ing the year were £5,935,625, being an increase of £115,107 over ,the year 1913. In, addition,' £10,315 was received in premiums under the Sickn'ess In- surance Tables. The claims of the ,year amounted to £4,014,058. The number of deaths was 9;351. The number of endowment assurances matured was 24,966, the premium income of which was £136.735. The number of policies in force at the end of the rear was 922,505. INDUSTRIAL BRANCH.—The premiums re- ceived during the year were £8,176,202, being an increase of £301,746. The claims of the year amounted to £3,373,850, including £398,360 bonus additions. The number of claitas and surrenders, inCluding 6,731 endowment assurances matured, was 392,883. The number of free policies granted dur- ing the year to those policyholders of five years' standing and upwards, who desired to discontinue their payments, was 103,514, the, number in force being 1,947,556. The number-of free policies which became claims during the year was 46,364. - The total number of policies in force in this Branch at the end of the year was 20,085,010; their average duration exceeds thirteen years. The assets of the Company, in both branches, as shown in the balance sheet, are £91,202,344, being an increase of £4,209,341 over those of 1913. The outbreak of hostilities in August last placed upon the Directors the grave responsibility of de- ciding what charge, if any, should be made to policyholders on active service. After careful con- sideration it was decided to charge no extra premium in respect of existing policies on the lives

of those engaging for the period of the war, and in respect of existing policies on the lives of other members of the Regular Forces it was decided that 250 of assurance on any life should be exempted from the payment of extra premium.. In the Ordinary Branch a reversionary bonus at , the rate of Al 103. per cent. on the original sums assured has been added to all classes of participat- ing policies issued since the year 1876. In the Industrial Branch a bonus addition will be made to the sums assured on policies of over five years' duration which become claims either by death or maturity of endowment from the sth of March; 1915, to the 2nd of March, 1916, both dates inclusive, as follows:

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, MARCH 13, 1915.

WILLING TO WOUND

• Again it is necessary for us to call attention to the manner in which some Conservatives are observing the party political truce which was, by common assent, arranged on the outbreak of the war. Perhaps it is expecting too' much of flesh and blood to expect everyo'ne, especially when in the invigorating atmosphere of a local,' Tory Club, to leave, the wicked Radi- cats altogether in peace. This week we have had -the annual meeting ofl .the Conservative Association, with Sir, GILBERT GREENILL in the chair, and Mr. W. PETER RYLANDS: as chief speaker—a 'well-worn programme to which long usage has accustomed us: There were great 'protestations regard- ing the existence Of, the "truce," but there was a further straining to get in party blows under cover of that truce. Some of -these attempts we have al- ready exposed. In the present case Sir GILBERT GREENALL led off by saying how proud the country was at .the present moment of the Opposition and the way in which. the Tory party had filled up the fighting ranks; and, so far as we can gather, there is no pride, in the Government and no mention of the ,Radicals who have gone to the trenches. But we do not. complain of Ihat, though we think the record as presented by Sir GiultaT might more properly have been left to the annual official report of the Secretary, Mr. GEORGE SMITH. What 'we complain about is the ungracious attack that .Mr. RYLANDS made upon his political opponents. The attack was made, of course, under the protection of the truce, and with a covering pretence of an appre- ciation of the extremely "proper and 'splendid way in which the Liberal party had thrown themselves into the matter of the war." But ..to Mr. RYLANI/S, the Radtcal party are what they are, at the present time because they are forced to act in the circum- stances as he would bave .ttem act. He quoted the famous couplet about the devil sick and the devil well, and it was reeeived with laughter—quite the regulation kind of laughter for one side to make at the expense of the 9ther side to an amalgamation of.,

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 13, 1915.

TERRITORIAL EQUIPMENT.

What Has Been Done and What Remains To Be Done. MRS. FAIRCLOUGH'S APPEAL.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—Your readers will be interested to know that a meeting was held in the Officers' Quar- ters, Drill Hall, Warrington, on Tuesday, March 9th, of ladies representing the officers of the two Battalions of the 4th South Lancashire Territorial Force. • The statement of work done was read, explain- ing that the Territorial Equipment Fund was started on August 25th, 1914, with the inten- tion of providing evety man with such comforts as might be requisitioned by the officer com- manding the Battalions. The financial statement was presented by the Hen. Treasurer, showing that the amount re- ceiyed, to date was £728 9s. 41d. of which sum A 1641 18s. 20. had been expended in the pur- chasing of shirts, socks, wool, and at Christmas, the providing., of a box of cigarettes to every man in both Battalions. The list of work done is, I am afraid, too long for the space you can give'me, but 5,093 articles have been sent to the Ist/4th Battalion, and 2,868 to the 2nd/4th Battalion. In addition, 500 pairs of socks and 250 shirts have been sent to the 2nd South Lan- cashire Regiment, through Mrs. treland-Black- burne, and 50 shirts and 100 iairs of socks, to- gether with £lO tit Christmas, to the Warrington Company of the A.S.C. Twenty pairs of blan- kets have also been forwarded.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
CHEERED BY HELP.

The committee have been greatly helped and cheered in their'work by the willing assistance of the wives of the non-commissioned officers, and the friends and well-wishers of the Batta- lions. The school children of Warrington, and district have taken no small share in these efforts, as they have knitted 1,000 pairs of mit- tens; as well as many socks. Mrs. Townroe has kindly undertaken the dis- tribution of wool amongst the wives:of the Ter- ritorials at Newton and EarlestOwn, and they have thus contributed 117 articles. Amongst many other gifts the .Thelwall Working Party has generously offered 400 pairs of socks. The committee feel sure that anyone studying

the statement of things supplied to Ur men will agree that the greatest possible care has been taken to provide the most necessary articles, and the expenditure will compare favourably with Ilkat of any other fund.

Seeing that -1(1/ 4th is full strength, it was generally f-elt, that the .whole of the ladies pres- ent should co-operate .in the work now being (lone, and it was unanimously decided that Mrs. J. Reid Walker, wife of the Honorary Colonel of the Battalion, should be asked to be President of the new committee. and I am' glad to say she has kindly' consented to do so. Mrs'. J. D. Fairclough and myfielf were elected •yiee:presi- dents, and the Hon. Treasurer, Mr. J. H. Sme- thurst, and the Hon. Secretary, Miss Helen Fairclbugh, remain- as before. The Committee ihnnk hll generous supporters 4. the fund, and 'will be glad of further help. either in money or kind, to enable them to serid weekly parcels to those at the Front, and, when required, The men training in the South. —Yours, &c., • •

ETHEL C. FAII3CLOTIcHL Drill Hall, Warrington, March 12th, 1915.

COMFORTS FOR OUR SOLDIERS.. GLOVES AND MITTENS.

Over Four Thousand Pairs Sen From Warrington.

To the Grand Duke, Michael well over four thousand pairs of gloves and mittens have now been forwarded from the John Crosfield Memorial Hall. The winter is now passing, and by the time these are worn out it is hoped that long-suffering "Tommy" will require no more for quite a time. Therefore the scheme will shortly close teraTiorarily. But "Tommy" has been hard OA to 16 'bear the rigours of the wintry campaign, and the welcome receipt of these coveted comfortsihae called forth his un- bounded gratitude.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
"4th SOUTH LAN CS."

For the 4th South Lancashires, now serving in the war zone, a consignment of 30 pairs has just departed en route for Major Crosfield, and all gloves and mittens which reach the Hall during the next week or two will also be sent direct to Warrington men. The class will; it is expected, finish work by the end of the month. The Richmond Gas Stove Company—one of the firms which has participated in the. scheme— has just sent in the last Jot of the gloves and mittens its workers have knitted, and has already received a letter of cordial congratula- tion from Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield—who have been supplying all the wool for the scheme. The company have also the honour of receiving the first Warrington letter from the Grand Duke Michael. This has been posted up in their works; they are very proud of it.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
A SPLENDID RECORD.

And the committee are very proud of the womenfolk who have worked so regularly and assiduously at their knitting. When it is recol- lected that practically every night they have been engaged on overtime labour, and yet have relaxed their efforts not a whit, one has -noth- ing but praise for them.

JUST JUSTICE. Magisterial Tribute to the Late Mr. William Long.

At the Dkiresbury Petty Sessions, held on Tuesday at Stockton Heath, the Chairman (Mr. F. A. Frost) said that since the last sitting of' that Court the Bench had suffered the loss by death of their greatly-respected and highly- esteemed colleague, Mr. William Long. Many of his benefactions were welt-known to all pre- sent, .and there were still more known only to a few. Thew present were concerned more par- ticularly with his life as an administrator of the law .and justice. He served in that Court for upwards of 36 years, during which time he administered justice with an even hand, and strictly kept his oath to do so without fear or favour .0)r .respect. of persons. A. more pains- taking magistrate could not be found any- where. He always threw the full weight of his influence on the side of leniency and mercy where such was possible, wishing to give an evil-doer the chance of a fresh start in life. He set an example which it was well worth the while of them, younger men, to try and follow. The Bench was the poorer by his loss, and he (the speaker) was sure they wished to extend to his widow and other members of his family their heattfelt sympathy in the hour of sorrow.

TRIBUTES FROM THE POLICE AND BAR.

Superintendent Owen said a, few words on behalf, of the police, mentioning that he had, known Mr. Long for the last twenty years.. Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, speaking for the soli- citors practising at the Court, also associated himself with the Chairman's remarks. /

CHESS CLUB SEASON.

The Warrington Chess Club conclnded their present season,on Thursday evening at Prince's Cafe, Sankey-street. The season has been a very successful one and the various competi- tions have been very keenly contested. Two matches have been played, one with Maccles- field and the other with Knutsford, but. in neither case were Warrington successful. The winners of the competitions were:— Club Trophy: 1, Mr. J. R. Wood; runner-up, Mr: H. C. Westmoreland. Second-class Competition': 1, Mr. F. S. Han- cock; runner-up, Mr. F. Black. • Handicap Tournament: 1, ,Mr. P. Black; runner-up, Mr. H. C. Westmoreland. There appears to be a considerable number of chess players in Warrington who seem tp be diffident about joining a club, but the com- mittee wish it to be known that novices in the game are equally welcome with those players of more advanced strength. Every assistance is given to, weaker players both in advice and practice, and they hope, therefore, that the next season will see a still further increase in the membership of the club.
