WAR STORIES: BY LETTER AND INTERVIEW.

lELI'ING TO ROUT THE TURKS.

Warringtonian Describes Sultan's Captured Men.

'RAG-TAG AND 808-TAIL" SOLDIERS.

Mr. George. Walker, of the Cheshire Lines Railway, Battersby-lane, Warrington, has re- ceived a letter from Lance-Sergeant B. Dick- son, one Of his men who joined the Manchester Regiment. Writing from near Cairo, Februa-y nth, Sergeant Dickson says : "No doubt you will have heard how I have been travelling about since -leaving the Railway. Company. On quitting , in England, and arriving at _ ~ we found that it was Egypt _ _ _

that we were hound for and not France, a fact which upset a good many of us. Well, re duly arrived at Alexandria During our

voyage out we met with a sight which we-shall nyt forget in a hurry. We.had just left Malta. behind when we Met the Indian Contingent sailing merrily for their destination Our voyage out was very slow, every precau- tion having to be observed. From Alex- .andria we were drafted to . There has been some fighting on the Suez Canal, and I was on escort .duty the other day, bringing Turkish prisoners in—three officers and two hundred men.

"A BROKEN-DOWN SET OF MEN."

"Of all ,the fighting_ men - I have ever seen

these were the limit. -A more broken-down set of men I have never set eyes on.' Some, it is true, had good clothing, but others had, hardly any' clothing at all, alld many of 'them were without boots. They had, we learned, been on the march ten days, and for the last five had hardly any food until they arrived at the canal bank. From What I can make out they were divided into to parties by their officers and were 'ordered to attack a point on the canal bank. One 'party, composed of half a Tabur (600 men) were ordered to attack Tassoum. They got "as far as the canal bank, where they were met with hot and well-aimed fire, which caused a great many casualties in their ranks. Then our troops surrounded them from behind and captured what remained of them. One German major was shot during the fighting, in which our casualties were very few'

===== 0002601_19150313_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
MAD WITH DELIGHT AT THE SIGHT OF

FOOD.

"A great many prisoners have been trought in 'here. The 4ot we brought in went 'mad Ath delight ,when they saw he barracks they were to be billeted in and the bedding they were to have, and, above all, at the food we gave them when they were all settled down. If there was ever A half-starved mob of fne,n it was this lot, and one could not help feeling sorry. for them; although they were outes ene- mies I am sure that they are glad

that they have fallen into our hands- and save no more fighting to do When in Cairo, I had a look round. The city,is a grand sight,, with its large white stone buildings, but se the real sights one has to go out a few miles, to the Pyraniids, for 'instance. Thert there Is Heliopolis,. a town which has sprung :up in the desert during the last few years, and is clean and picturesque and somehow reminds one of Chester. Yet anaher place to visit, if one has the time, is Sakhara, where is the graveyard a the ancient kings' of Memphis. Leaving the station ~at a placb called Ghiza, one arrives at-Badrashein•

IN THE FOOTSTEPS OF THE PHARAOHS. "There Dne finds it necessary to requisition donkeys for the remainder of the journey, and they can be hired at a reasonable charge. Very fine scenery is traversed on the road, but probOly the Most interesting of the roadside sights are the. two Collosii of Rameses the Second. One has to climb the two great figures, now prostrate, to appreciate the splendid.work- manship displayed by the long dead and gone Pgyptian sculptors. One can only marvel that men, so many thousands of years ago, could have wrought such .wonders,• considering the primitive tools at the disposal of workers in stone at the time these giant figures were carVed. The road to Salkbara passes through ruined Memphis. All, at remains of the ancient city is a maze of mud-walls, thick enough almost to form roadways for carriages to travel along. A ONCE PROUD CITY.

"Standing amid the ruins of the long dead city, one could not help trying to imagine some- thing of the lives and doings of the people of the days when these ruined walls made up the great centre from which all Egypt was ruled. Beyond Memphis, one passes through tracts of cultivated country and a number of quaint villages, most of the residents of which turn but to stare at one. At length one arrives at the vast graveyard of Sakliftra, and, using that as ,a base, so to' speAk, one scouts around, and in turn visits 1-)tahotep, Mera Kagoumi (which is k whole street of ancient tombs), the Sero- peum and Ti. By far the most interesting of the sights is that afforded .by the Seropeum, or the Tomb of the Sacred Bulls. Here may be -seen, on either side of a deep and dark subter- ranean passage cut out of the solid rock, the twenty-four sarcophagii, which were intenaed, at some remote period in the history of the land of mystery, to hold and hide for ever the bodies of the Sacred Bulls. Now, however, they are free from mummified remains, history telling us that they were despoiled at the time of the Arabian invasion.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
PAINT THAT THE CENTURIES HAVE NOT

DIMMED.

"The carved and painted pictures on the walls of every tomb indicate a wonderful degree of art among the ancient craftsmen. Though many, many centuries have passed since they were sculptured and painted, these mural tablets are in an almost perfect state of preservation, the colouring of all .being still good, and, of some, hardly touched by time. These pictures represent the occupations of the ancient .Euptians-Artinting, fishing, building; in fact, every branch of their sport and indus- try is depictured in paint and sculptured stone. . . . It was a great outing and the two or three miles' journey that we made on the cars, during one part of the trip, only cost the equivalent of a half-penny in English money." _ _ _

In a postscript the writer adds: "Every man here is getting plenty :if work to do."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
ONE KILLED, THREE WOUNDED.

Private Mulcahy's Experiences %%id Warrington Territorials.

Word from Private John Mulcahy—who, With several of his fellow workers from Dal- lam Forge boileryard, enlisted in the Territorials about five months ago—this week ,reached his father, Mr. Maurice Mulcahy, of 137, Orford-street, Warrington. " Keep Our Heads Down."

"I am the pink," writes the gallant private. . . . " I suppose you will _be

Anxious for me. Well, you need have no fear. If we keep our heads down we are pretty safe. The shells are dangerous, but they nearly alwayi go over our heads. We went in the trenches last Saturday night for the first time, and did 24 hours. We went in again' on Monday night, and we go in again to-night till to-morrow night. Then we have a four-days' rest—and I want it, for this is a tiring job. "We had one killed and three, wounded last Monday. The man killed was only about three Yards from where I stood. The bullet hit him in the head—he was getting up too often. . . . We go in the trenches with Regulars. There are some rum blokes among them; they think nothing of getting on top of the trench to have a look at the Germans. I am glad to say H. Dodd is doing well: He does not go in the same times as me, and when we come back we look for one another\ Catholic Church Only Place Not Hit.

"I suppose you will have been waiting for a letter. , Well, I wrote one last Friday, but could not post it.. . . . I am writing this in a Catholic Church, which we sleep, in when we are not in the trenches. It's about the only place in the village that has not been hit by shells. This must have, been a splendid place before the Germans came. Now everywhere in it has got a shell through it. I was talking to a fellow who was in the same regiment as Mahone, and he said they didn't kno* whether he was killed Or taken prisoner. . . I can't find wh'dre the Munsters are. They don't seem to be about here. . . . We are glad to get a letter, so write often. . . ."

. .

===== 0002601_19150313_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
A FEW- LINES FROM CEYLON. Sergeant Hopes Orford Men "Will Make A Name For Themselves."

Writing to the Vicar of Orford (the Rev. G. Vale,Owen) Company-Sergeant Major• Samuel Clam, who is stationed at Gallo Face, Colombo, Ceylon, and who is a native of Warrington, says "All the regular troops are very keen to get home to the fighting. . . . We were very anxious while the 'Emden' was about here and all hoped that she would pay us a visit. One feels it keenly when you read in the papers of the rough times our comrades-in- arms are having at the Front, and we have not the chance of going to assist in'the great struggle. . . . I am thankful for my train- ing at the dear old village Day and Sunday Schools at Orford; those were, very happy times. Isn't the patriotism of the Colonies splendid? It was a treat to see the Australian Contingent here a few weeks ago. . . . I suppose a lot of young fellows in Orford have joined the New Army, and I hope they .will make a name for themselvesland for Orford:"

===== 0002601_19150313_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
STRENUOUS AND EXCITING LIFE.

Shelled Out Of Their Billets.

An officer of our Territorial Battalion now at the Front, writes as .followv-- " The Germans are trying to locate our own 'big guns, and we get the benefit of their shells about twice daily for a period varying each time from half an hour to an hour. "We have done several days' duty in the trenches; and we are deeply sorry to have lost Private Watson, a man highly respected and who will be sadly missed. We have also had four other men wounded, but in no case are the wounds serious.

"The little house in which we are billeted

has, not a complete window in,it. We have no fire S except for cooking and get what rest we can fully dressed" and ready to turn out at a moment's notice. For instance, one morning reveille was at 5 a.m. We marched at 8.30 a couple of miles to a permanent rest camp. Our company which were for duty in the trenches had an open field to stay in, ankle deep in mud. We marched away about 7 p.m. and arrived at our present home about 9 p.m. After going round some reserve trenches at 12 p.m. lwe finished .about 4 a.m. At 5 a.m. we stood to arms, during the whole of the time the frost being' very keen. About 11 a.m. the-following day we had to clear out of our billets because of the shell fire, and at 7.45 p.m. we again left for the trenches from which we 'returned the 'following night at 10 p.m. it is a strenuous and exciting life, but we manage somehow to keep up our spirits right well under most distressing circumstances."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
MOUTH-ORGANS IN TIIE TRENCHES How "Mr., Atkins" "Keeps His Pecker Up."

This month's Orford Parish Magazine con- tains an interesting letter written to the Vicar (the Rev. G. Vale-Owen) by Corporal James Swinton, whose. home is in Long-row, Orford, and who is on active service with his regiment. Dozens of Dead Germans in Front of the

Trenches.

There are dozens of Germans dead in front of our trenches. It is a pity we cannot get to them and bury them. The way we are working now is, we do four days in and four days out; .so it is not too bad. When the weather does improve no doubt we shall move the Germans out of it. It is a very good thing. The Rus- sians.are doing very goodwork. . . . . How glad I am to receive your letters and the Church portrait, too. , It is lovely,tand it does remind us of the Old Homeland. Well, I am in the trenches now, and the Germans are oniy about 200 yards in front of us-', and they are very quiet during the day; it seems as thou:;i they were tired of shooting at times. But it is at night the sniping.goes on. They have fetched a few of our fellows down, too. But never mind, there is One above who is on guard with us, and He watches us wherever we go. He is the Silent Sentry through the night..

"Our Fellows Are All Cheerful."

"Well, all our fellows are cheerful; always singing and laughing and playing mouth- organs, too. We go in the trench .for four days and then we go in billets for four days' rest, and we always look forward to these days to come. The Germans generally start playing an old cornet. During the night they have been shelling an old farm just behind us. They are a very artful lot. They have a big searchlight, too, and they send rockets up, and we have to keep low or else we shall, get hit. Well, I am happy, and I hope we shall not be long before we are ,all back safe. I am writing this from the forward position, with shells bursting all around. But the Germans seem to me to be thrpwing ammunition away; they are shelling farms and cottages that are, empty. I sup- pose they, are trying to locate our guns.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
A Narrow Eschpe.

The country is very muddy and wet at present. I have never seen so much mud in my life. Not much news here; things are about the same; only the last time in the trenches, the Germans threw about six grenades into! our trench, but no one was injured. They go off just like a bomb. And I had a narrow shave myself; 'walking about, not thinking, when a bullet went past my head and put a ringing in my ears that I felt for half-an-hour. So shall have to be a bit careful in future."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
2nd TERRITORIAIL BATTALION.

Brigadier Congratulates Colonel Fairelough.

The `44th Battalion, South Lancashire •Regi- ment, who_ are now undergoing a severe train- ing in the South of England, were inspected on Wednesday by the Brigadibr-General. The Battalion mustered at full strength, every avail- able man being on parade. Forming up "in mass" on a.large common in the vicinity, the men waited the.arrival of the inspecting officer, who was received with a general salute. The General walked up and down each rank and minutely /inspected the men, stopping now and then to ask a man some particulars of his service and other questions. • After the inspection, he addressed a few iwords of congratnlation to the Battalion. He said he was struck with the steadiness of the men on parade. That was a tine example of discipline, but they must work hard and be as good a battalion as that now serving their Icing and country on foreign soil. He had been very proud to have the lst/ 4th South Lancashires under his conimand f9r several years, and he wished the 2iid Battalion to follow in their foot- steps. Although they had not all volunteered for Imperial service, one never knew when there would be a sudden dash, on- our shores, and it behoved every officer and man to be prepared to meet this emergency.

- lie concluded with the words" Colonel Fair- dough, I congratulate you." The men generally are finding life in empty houses a great change from their comfortable billets at Blackpool, but all are satisfied to "put up" with a little discomfort to, make themselves fit to meet the Kaiser's hordes. The people make the troops very welcome, and are doing their best to make the life of their khaki visitors as pleasant and comfortable as possible.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR BELGIAN GUESTS.

The Belgian refugees whom Warrington has honoured herself .by making her guests con- tinue to be sympathetically cared for and well housed. One needs no further proof of that than the smiling faces which greet the visitor to either the Bold-street. or Bewsey-street hostels, in which they are quartered. The refugees are intensely appreciative of even the smallest kindness shown them, and, as one lady interested in- them said to our represen- tative the other day, "It is a pleasure to do something for people who are so genuinely, and yet not obsequiously, grateful." The. commit- tee who have the care of the guests in hand have succeeded in placing some of the married couples into private ,apartments, and the men are, as opportunity offers, obtaining employ- ment, and thus becoming self-supporting. All the refugees that our representative as spoken to are delighted with Warrington—it is so big and so busy and so kind, they say—and they are so hill of gratitude to the people who have come to their aid in their hour of sore need.

-

===== 0002601_19150313_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
ALARMING RUMOURS.

The Mayor Warns People of Danger of Loose Talk.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—lt ha 's come to my, knowledge that cer- tain evil-minded persons have been mgking and circulating false reports concerning the men of the 4th Battalion of the South Lancashire Regi- ment now serving at the Front. These reports have caused quite unnecessary anxiety in the town, and given great alarm and mental suffering to those who have sent their dear ones to the war. _ _ .

Instead of causing grief and pain, it is our duty to show the greatest kindness and forbear- ance to those who are going through such try ing times, and I regard it as my duty to do everything in my power to suppress and put an end to these wicked and mischievous reports.

I, therefore, ,call public attention to the regu- lations made under the Derence of the Realm Act, and particularly to the following:- 21. No person shall by word of mouth or in writing spread reports likely to cause dis- satisfaction or alarm among any of His Majesty's Forces or among the civilidn popula- tion.

Persons contravening this regulation will be

lif.ble to imprisonment. I have given instructions that all false re- ports shall, if possible, be brought home to the offenders, but I hope that, after this warning, the inhabitants Of the trwn will refrain from circulating reports about the men at the Front or the Battalion without first ascertaining the truth thereof.—Yours faithfully, PETER PEACOCK, Mayor.

WARRINGTONIANS IN THE ANTIPODES. Ilm% They Have Answered " The Call."

Pte. W. Roe, who 'is a member of Crosfield's Ambulance Brigade, and is at present attached to the Royal Naval Sick Berth Reserve, and is doing duty at the Stonehouse (R.N.) Hospital, Plymouth, writes an interesting letter to a friend in Warrington. He says that when be was in Australia, prior to being drafted back to England, he came across some old Warrington lads, who had answered the country's call, and were coming back to fight for, her with the Antipodean contingent. Among those he met was Wallace Barratt, the son of Will Barratt, "the old Warrington forward." He came over in the same ,boat as Wallace Barrag and a de- tachment of his comrades, who &tide up the first Australian Expeditionary Force. "Wal- lace," says the writer, "had joined the Vic- torian Infantry, and I was surprised when I met him on the deck just as the gangways were drawn."

PINING FOR A "GO" AT THE GERMANS. " Ile,and his chums were in good health and spirits, and were looking forward to having a "go" at the Germans. . . . They all looked forward to seeing the old country; indeed, you might have thought them all English born if you had heard the frequency with which they used the phrase "going home!" They always referred to England as home. . . I have just received a postcard from Wallace Bartatt, and he asks to be remembered to his friends in Warrington, and those of them Who are at Messrs. Fletcher, Russell's in particular. . . . . _

I am sending him 'The Examiner,' and I would like him to see that I have let his friends

know that he is doing well.

FAREWELL CONCERT TO NORTI LANCASHIRES.

A Merry Evening At Orford Harm/is.

Last week-end a detachment of men belong- ing to the Loyal North Lancashire Regiment left the town. For• some few days they had been quartered, in Orford Barracks and the night before they left they attended an enjoy- able smoking concert in the Barracks. The concert was arranged by R.S.-M. Quick and Sergeant W. McDonald (of the 2nd South Lan- cashires). _ _ _ _

The programme opened with a band selection —" Tommy's" favourite marching song, "It's a long way to Tipperary "—which was ap- plauded to•the echoes. Next, Drummer Hughes sang "The Magic of your voice" and "The blind boy," both efforts being greeted with loud applause, as were also the gramophone selec- tions reeled off on Mr. Lambert's "Music— Machine." The band followed with another inspiriting number—a selection from " Tann- ,hauser," including, of course, the stirring and immortal march music. Miss Hewitt delighted the company with a sweet rendition of "The Rosary!' Private Aspinall contributed the sotag "Nobody Wants to marry me," and two rousing concertina solos, and Company Sergt.- Major Campbell sang :‘`Just a song at twilight." , Mr. Carl Dixon mystified and amused the com- pany with a number of clever conjuring tricks, and Mr. Lambert recited Kipling's "Hindoo's Paradise." Private Lloyd's sentimental song, "Some day you'll forgive and forget," was a big success. The programme having been brought to a close with the singing of the National Anthem, the " boys " sat down to an excellent hot supper, to which full justice was done. It should be added that the band, which did so much to liven up the proceedings, was under the conductorship of Mr. Cahill. Company. Sergt.-Major Campbell, it is inter- esing to recall, was the leader of the regiment's theatrical troupe during their days On foreign service.

COMFORTS FOR "MR. ATKINS." Junior Liberals He'psi° Swell "Gloves

and Alitt" Fund.

In order to assist the fund opened to pro- vide working parties of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association with materials out of which to continue their good work of making woollen gloves and mittens and other much-needed and, much-prized comforts for the men at the Front and in training, the com- mittee of the Junior Liberal Club—now, in its re-constructed form, "going strong"; in fact, daily growing in strength—have arranged a series of whist drives and dances. The first of the series was held in the Crosfield Memo- rial Hall on Thursday evening, and was an unqualified success, close upon 250 young people being present. The pleasure of the success was heightened from the fact that this was the first social event organised by the Juniors since the reconstruction scheme came into operation. The prizes for whist at Thursday's social. event were the gift of Councillor Dickenson, and they were .graciously handed to the win- ners by the Mayoress,. who was accompanied by her husband, the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock).

Mr. George Toulmin moved a vote of thanks to the Mayoress for giving the prizes away, to Councillor Dickenson for donating the same, and to Mrs. Osborne and her band of workers from the Women's Association who had helped to make/the function a success and who gave and dispentsed the refreshments. Mr. Peter Peacock, jun., seconded the pro- position, which Was carried with acclamation. Mr. Peacock, jun. is now the secretary of the Junior Club arid Association, the gentle- man who was recently elected to that position (Mr. Abraham Hoyle) having gallantly en- listed a few days after his appointment. After the presentation came dancing, the music for which was supplied by Mr. S. N. Tomkinson.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

FIRST-RATE MELODRAMA AT THE ROYAL COURT.

A singularly well-written melodrama holds the boards at the Royal Court Theatre this week, in the 'shape of "The Maid of the Mill." The piece, which grips the attention of the audience from the rise to the fall of;the cur- tain, deals with Lancashire life and is mainly in the dialect so familiar to Warrington ears. The plot runs on original lines and the author, Mr. E. Vivian Edmonds, is to be congratulated upon his effort. The play is excellently-acted, Mr. Eric Norman giving a forceful portrayal of the part of Ned, whilst Miss Gladys Ford- Howitt acts with great charm as his daughter Mary—the "Maid of the Mill." The part of the stern father, Adam Ackroyd, is effectively delineated by Mr. Ernest C. Edwards, and Miss Millicent Dalzell acts most convincingly as the villainous step-mother, the rest of the long cast being most capably filled.

War Play Next Week.

Twice nightly next -week patrons of the Royal Court are to have the opportunity Of witnessing a thrilling war play. Entitled "In Time of War," it is from the pen of that master melo- dramatist, C. Watson Mill, and it will be played by a first-class company headed by Miss Alida L. Lindon. The play promises to-be full of sen- sational incidents.

STAR KINEMA ARMY FILMS.

The ,very attractive bill of fare presented during the first half of the week commenced with "The Capture of the Kaiser," a topical comedy showing the famous 'Pimple at his best. "The Hand of Iron" was a very fine drama of love and hate in the Army. ," Pathe's Ani- mated Gazette" this time illustrated the work of the Boy Scouts during the war. "Yuletide in Holland "—both scenic and educational— was very interesting. Thal delightful Army film, "Bootle's Baby," paid a welcome return visit by special request. "'[HE PRUDENTIAL."

A Great Insurance Office and the War.

To all interested in life assurance, says "The Insurance Mail," particularly as it affects the public at large, the great event of the year is the publication of the Prudential's annual report. Everybody in the business looks up to the Prudential not only as repre- senting in itself about one-half of a big national movement, but as a standard or a , model. Its annual report is , scanned with interest by all. 4110%is year it is of even more interest than usual, for it answers a question which many folks have been asking for some months: How has the war affe3ted the kru- dential? The answer is a very encouraging one. It is true that the ordinary and industrial branch bonuses have been reduced—because of the temporary depreciation in the value of securities, and the increased claims and lia- bilities all brought about by the war. But the income, far from showing a decrease in the worst crisis the business has ever seen, has actually made a phenomenal increase. 1913 was an exceptionally good year, but 1914 was a better as regards premium income. Thus in the ordinary branch an increase of one hun- dred and fifteen thousand pounds has been made in 1914 against an increase of ninety- three thousand pounds in 1913. In our opinion this increase is the feature of the year. It shows very clearly the supreme confidence of the public in the Prudential. When other securities were distrusted and the financial world was in a state bordering on chaos, the public put its money to an increasing extent in the Prudential. The company can ask for no greater tribute than that; nor can the pub- lic give a better sign of good sense.

SOME BIG FIGURES. Other securities have been falling rapidly in capital value: there is no fear of an issurance policy in a reputable office decreasing in value. The money put in is safe to the last farthing. Turning to the industrial branch we see an even larger increase in premium income, as the following short table ghows:— Industrial Branch • Premium Income. 191 f.? 1913 1914 Thus, whereas the public paid to the Pru- dential in weekly or 'smonthly premiums ..{:7,874,456 in such a good year as 191 a, it paid .0,176,202 ,in 1914—five months of which were months of war. Moreover, whereas the in- crease in industrial branch premium income was ,t 81,893 in 1913, it was .001,746 in 1914! In the whole history of the c.ompany only two years have shown a greater growth namely, in 1906 there was an increase of three hundred and sixty thousand pounds, and in 1883 there was an increase of three hundred and seventy- eight thousand pounds. This is a very won- derful fact in view of the .serious effect of the declaration of war upon the industrial classes and is a further tribute to. the trust of those classes in the Prudential. The other feature of the report is the enormous sums the direc- tors have put aside, out of profits, to strengthen the • position of the company. The extra liabilities imposed by the war have been provided for in that way. The company therefore is as safe as a rock and as sound as .a bell.

The—.3ledical Officer of Health's weekly re- Port. gives the estimated population of War- rington at present as being 75,532. During the seVen clays ended March 6th 52 births and 28 deaths were registered-in-the town, as against 41 and 34 respectively in: the 'corresponding Period of last year. Of 'the deaths last week seven were those of chilren under five and eleven of persons between the ages of 25 and 65, whilst seven were those of old people of 65 and over. Emmanuel Fellows' Study Circle.

On Sunday morning Mr. J. Brown gave a 'Paper entitled "Music in Church Life," to the 'Members of the above circle. Mr. Brown etated that church music had a great past, a "great present, and a still greater future. Very 'little church music such as that played by the band of -Saul on instruments as the sackbut, Psaltery, lute and harp, bad survived, but still, enough to show that if it were performed ,to- (tay it would touch no chords of sympathy in the hearts of the bearers. It would have very little effect. yet this was the music which comforted wing Saul in his rage and madness, and also inspired the magnificent music poetry of the Psalms. They, to-day, offered to God a thing of beauty upon which their talents and energies should be expended, to render is as little unworthy of its object as might be. Music had an influence which no one could explain and no one could deny. What was it that made -people sing so heartily in ehureh? It was not the instrument, but it was the spirit Which they put into the song, for only he who Praised. God- most heartily, praised Him with his best. Referring to the verse in St. John c4nd is a spirit, and they that worship Him must worship Him in spirit and in truth," the speaker remarked that the essence Of their Pra roust he spiritual. Mr. Brown concluded 03" stating that a man's energies could not be better occupied than in seeking to return to the Giver of all beauty the best be could pro- Iluce of those forms of beauty which the human brain was able tp create upon earth; pointing out that all arts were employed in the service of God—architecture, painting, &c. —but all differing from music, in that they Lemained in a-state of repose until destroyed Y time. Music, on the other hand, was active and living. Its message could.. only be conveyed to the world by living agents inter- preting it.

Improvement Schemes "Huff Ur

The Town Clerk has received a Com— munica7 tion from' the Local Government announcing that in accordance with a decision of the Treasury no capital moneys must be borrowed by Corporations without its approval, and that that approval will not be given to any' Cor- poration for the purpose of raising lOans for any work which is not actually in mgress, though the sanction of the Board may have been obtained to such work. Locally, that means that the Warrington Bridge and But- termarket-street, widening schemes may be completed, as they are works actually in pro- gress, but several other contemplated imprOve- ments must be "hung up,-" such as the new river wall opposite the Weir; the Market Gate improvement; the extension of the electricity supply to Stockton Heath and other places; the new water scheme about which so much has been heard rticently in the Council Chamber; the eitension of the Gas Works; the new Sanatorium; the new Smallpox Hospital and new schools, other than those in course of erection.

PREPARATORY TRAINING CORPS.

At a largely-attended meeting in Parr Hall on Thursday night the claims of • the Warrington and District Preparatory Training_ Corps an.-1 kindred volunteer organisations were explained. Mr. J. Fulton-Smith presided, and Supporting him on the platform were the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock)—who was to have taken the chair, but was detained until late at a Meeting Of the Insurance Committee—the Rev. Canon Willis (Rector of Warrington), the Deputy Mayor (Dr. Joseph), Sir Gilbert Greenall, Aldermen Ben- nett and Smethurst, Colonels Moggridge, Afocatta and Lyon, Capt. H. Fairclough, Coun- cillor Jenkins, Dr. Peacocke, and Messrs. Wm. Furness, W. H. Bellian, H. Hayes, Hy. Collin, John Coxon, P. H. Houghton and E. England. Mr. A. 11. Crosfield wrote: "The spirit in the ,00untry •at ..present _is. aplendid—it was nevtr better! Everyone is honestly and whole-heart- edly anxious to 'do his bit,' and, therefore, whilst our men of military age have given a splendid and patriotic response to the call ,to arms in defence of the realm,, one can well understand the anxiety of the men who have passed that age to render service to the State at home."

Other letters regretting inability to attend were received from the Rev. John Yonge and Mr. George Dakin.

The Ch-airman, speaking on the question of eligibility, said that last week he met an offi- cer from the War Office, who told him that it would be left to the discretion and loyalty of those, at the !lead of the movement to examine excuses as might be put forward, and, if they were satisfied that the reasons advanced were genuine, and that there ivas no .question of shirking, the War Office would accept their de •ision and allow the man, or men, in ques- tion to join the training corps. Mr. Hadfield addressed the meeting at length on the aims of the Central Association of Vol- unteer Training Corps, and said it had two main objects in view. The first was to get re- cruits for the Regular Army. (Applause.) All fit men of military age ought to join the Army, and ought to join at once. (Hear, hear.) The other object the Association had in view was to afford an outlet to the energies, the abilities and the desires of those men who were over the military age.

ROLL OF HONOUR.

LOCAL LANCE-CORPORAL KILLED IN ACTION.

Another Warringtonian has died a hero's death at the Front—Lance-Corporal 'Henry Sykes, of the 2nd Northumberland Fusiliers. The sad news was conveyed a few days ago in a letter from the War Office to the soldier's parents—Mr. and Mrs. Edward Sykes, of 25, vv illis-street. This letter stated that he hau been "killed in action on February 18th." Lance-Corporal Sykes, who was unmarried, was born—lt youngest son—in Warrington 23 years ago, and received his education at the National Schools under the headmastership of Mr. J. Dewhurst. Five years ago he enlisted, and two years afterwards went out with his regiment to India, 'where he remained on for- eign service until December. His regiment was then. recalled to Europe to join the Allied Armies in France, and en route 'called at Eng- land. 'The Lance-Corporal seized the chance to visit his home, which he reached about Christmas time, and where he spent a five days' leave. _ _ _

To France the 2nd Northumberlands sailed on January 15th; and after only two days at the base entered the trenches. Thus they had been exactly one month in the firing line when the gallant soldier laid down his life for King and country. Two other sons of Mr. and Mrs. Sykes are in the Army, holding the respective ranks of Ser- geant-Major and Pioneer-Sergeant.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
SERGEANT'S TRIWCITE TO LATE PRIVATE GREEN.

During the week official confirmation of the death of Rifleman Edmund Green—who, as we reported last week; was shot through the head by a German sniper, whilst engaged in trench work—has been received by tie gallant soldier's sorrow-stricken mother, Mrs. Green, of Mersey View .332, Knutsford-road. The intimation was conveyed on the usual printed form over the signature "Kitchener," and containing the words, after stating the fact of the rifleman's death, "The King commands me to assure you of the true sympathy of his Majesty and the Queen in your sorrow." Sergeant's Sympathy.

Mrs. Green ha; also received a Communication from Sergt. H. Osborne, of A, Company, of the 6th (Reserve) Battalion of the Rifle Brigade—in which tlf dead soldier served—saying: ." I re- gret to inform you that Rifleman Green was killed in action on February 15th, 1915, and beg to tender my deepest sympathy with you in the loss of your son, who was a well-conducted and promising soldier. I have taken the liberty of distributing the contents of the parcel, which you sent him, .amongst his comrades, which action I hope will meet with your approval."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH. 13, 1915.

TERRITORIAL ROLL OF HONOUR.

First List of Killed and Wounded.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
FACING DEATH IN THE TRENCHES,

The Warrington Terrltorials, the 4th Bat- talion South Lancashire Regiment, have now been in the trenches some time, and the price of patriotism is being paid, as is shown with solemn emphasis by the casualties that are being reported. Up to the present the Terri- torial Roll of Honour includes the following:•

KILLED IN ACTION. Private Thomas Watson. Private Albert Hughes.

DIED OF WOUNDS RECEIVED IN ACTION Private Private Briscoe.

WOUNDED. Private Richard Smith. Private E. T. Kinsley. Private J. Mercer. Private F. Holmes.

letters, Pte. Edgar • Booth, a bandsman and stretcher-bearer with the Territorials, begins his missive to his mother, who lives at 22, Rhode-street, with the words: "Tom Watson lhas been killed here," and goes on to say,: • "Your litter of this morning found me just behind the firing line, where we wait to take our turn in the trenches. I have done one 24 hours' spell in the trenches, and am going again to-night. The first time I went I had the mis- fortune to carry a dead man out, and it wasn't a nice job. especially with bullets whizzing past like rain. I am doing well, except for a slight cold. We have some exciting tithes, but will soon get used to it." t

The writer of the other letter referred to says hat Watson was tuned in a churchyard.

PRIVATE A. .HUGHES SHOT WHILST FIRING AT ENEMY

On Tuesday morning the news reached War- rington that another gallant • member of the Battalion had laid down his life for his country. He was Private Alfred Hughes, and he served in the A Company The news of his death was conveyed to hil parents, Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Hughes—who liVe at 34, Bewsey-road—in the following, letter, written by Major Cary, and dated March sth :

It is my sail duty to inform you 'that your son has been killed in action on the 3rd inst. On behalf of myself, officers, non-commis- sioned officers and men of A Company, may I 'offer you and your family our sincere sym- pathy. It may be some consolation to you in your sorrow to know that your son died like a British soldier, facing the enemy, and at the time of his death was actually firing his rifle.

PRIVATE T. WATSON SHOT THROUGH THE HEAD.

So far as is at. present known, Private Thomas Watson was the first of the Warrington Terri- torials to lose his life in the service of his country. in the present war. The news of this gallant warrior' § fate reached the town last Saturday morning, and was contained in a hur- riedly-written letter addressed to a sister, which

===== 0002601_19150313_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
ONE OF THE FIRST WARRINGTONIANS TO VOLUNTEER.

Private Thomas Watson was 38 years of. age, and he was unmarried. He was educated at the National Schools, Church-street, Warrington, and after leaving school went to work in the local L. and N.-W. Railway loco. sheds, where he continued to work up till comparatively recently, when he migrated to Widnes, where he worked at the Lancashire Metal Works, lodg- ing with his uncle, Mr. Thomas Dainteth, in Prince's-street, Widnes. Some years ago he joined the 4th (Territorial) Battalion of the. South Lancashire Regiment, and when the call came for volunteers for active service he was among the first to put down his name. He left Warrington with the contingent who were re- cruited by the late Major Applkon, and who first went to Edinburgh, but were subsequently drafted down South to complete their war train- ing, and crossed over to France some three weeks before the private met his-fate.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
A FRIENDLY SOCIETY MAN.

The dead private took an interest in Friendly Society work, and was a member of the " Con- stitutional " Lodge of the Independent Order of Oddfellows, of which his father has for many years been the treasurer. • When ever on fur- lough about a week before being drafted to France, he attended a meeting of the lodge. He had a chat with Mr. T. S. Steel, P.P.G.M., who is a trustee of the lodge, and who expressed the wish that the gallant Territorial would go on all right whilst away and would get safely through the war. Watson replied that he felt that he would get through all right, and said he was only tea anxious to get to the Front, and have the chance of accounting for a, few Ger- mans before he came back. A third party in the conversation suggested that it would not be long before peace was restored, whereupon Wat- son jocularly remarked- that he hoped- there' would be no peace before he had had an oppor- tunity of getting into the firing line.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRST NAME ON ODDFELLOWS' SCROLL

OF HONOURED DEAD.

One of the questions discussed at that night's lodge meeting was that of having a Roll of. Honour on which to inscribe the names of the members of the lodge who might fall in, the war. No definite decision was come to on • the matter, difference of opinion arising as to whether each lodge in the district should .have its separate Roll of Honour, or whether it would not be better to have one Roll for all the local lodges. Little did they think, as Mr. Steel re- marked in the course of a letter of sympathy to the deceased's father, that the private who sat amongst them as they debated the matter, and who took a Small part in the discussion, would be the first to have his name inscribed on the suggested Roll, when it takes shape. Kindly and cheery in his disposition, Private Watson was an immense favourite with his com- rades, who had dubbed him "Uncle."

ONLY TWENTY-ONE YEARS OLD.

• Private Hughes was only 21 years of age. He received his education at the Heathside School, and upon leaving went to an insurance office nnd then into Messrs. Joseph Cros- field and Sons' office as a clerk. He was very popular with his fellow clerks, and won the praise of those over him by his diligent ap- plication to duty. .40in all-round athlete, he was particularly interested in cricket and football, and 'was an active member of the Emmanuel Church Sports Club. • He ,figured in that, club's cricket team last season, and played football for them so recently as five weeks ago, when he was home on fur- lough, prior to being drafted to France.

At one time he was connected with the Buck- ley-street Methodist Church. He was a member of the International Order of Good Templars for many years, first as a juvenile, in the "Young Missipn" Temple, and afterwards as a member of the senior Lodge, "Temperance Mission," in which he had held various offices.

MAYOR'S SYMPATHY.

Private llinjli Died "A Glorious

Death."

The parents have received the following sym- pathetic letter from the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), dated from the Mayor's ' Parlour, Town Hall, on March 10: "Dear Mr. Hughes,— I am very sorry indeed to learn of your son's death, and I beg to offer you and yours my deepest sympathy. "I can quite understand how you must feel the loss—l have a son now at the Front—and 1. sincerely trust you will bear up under your trouble, and be fortified somewhat by the glorious thought that his wase noble death.

"With rill good wishes, I remain, sincerely yours, 1.

"Teter Peacock, Mayor."

Captain Gordon Roberts was . received at Private Weldon's home. It ran as follows :

"I am very soiry to have to inform you that your son, Private J. Weldon, who was wounded on Sunday last, died in hospital on March 7th. He received a rifle wound, being shot in the face and neck. At the time he was fully conscious, and news of his death to-day came as a surprise. We all expected to hear of his rebovery. You have the satisfaction of knowing that he died for a great cause, nobly doing his duty; and you have the deepest sympathy of myself, and, in fact, all of us."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
"DIED LIKE THE BRAVE SOLDIER s HE WAS."

Messrs. Crosfield's Condolences.

The bereaved parents have also received the following letter of sympathy frOin Councillor R. Roberts, who, writing on behalf of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, the deceased's for- mer employers, says :—" May , I offer on behalf of my chairman (who is away) and colleagues, our very deepest symiathy in the very sad b-ereaveinent which has befallen you and your family. Your son has given his life for our country and has died like the brave soldier- he undoubtedly was. I pray that the Great Consoler may give to you all that com- fort and strength,, which He alone can bestow., in abundant measure. Time can assuage the intensity of yinar• present grid, but some day, I hope before long, you will recover from the bitterness .of this great sorrow, and will find much satisfaction in the knowledge that your son fell while doing his duty—faithful to the end."

PRIVATE WELDON PASSES AWAY IN HOSPITAL.

Private J. Weldon, whose home was in Egerton-street, Howley, has died of wounds

received in action. The deceased, who was highly spoken of by all who knew' him—" right good lad" was the opinion of his inti- mates—was single and was only 26 years of age. Some time after leaving school he en- tered Messrs. Rylands' works, and, at the time of volunteering his services for the Tronti he was employed there as a wiredrawer.

PRIVATE W. BRISCOE ALSO DIES FROM WOUNDS.

Offered Tltree Times Before He Was 'Listed.

News ha S' been received by Mr. and Mrs. Briscoe 4 of 37.7, Wellfield-street, that their son, Private W. Briscoe, has also died of wounds. Only 22 years of age, Private Briscoe was of the stuff that heroes are made of. When the war • broke Dut he .went off to the Recruiting Office to offer himself, and was greatly dis- appointed when he was rejected by .the doctor. He was then suffering from a slight affection, which caused him to limp. A few weeks later Private Briscoe made a second attempt to enlist, but again had the mortification of being rejected. Nothing daunted, he went to the Recruiting 'StatiDn a third time, .and, to his great joy, was then passed as fit for,service.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
FATAL BULLET PASSED THROUGH POCKET TESTAMENT.

The news of the plucky private's death was conveyed to his parents in a letter written by the Rev. Harold T. A. -Pearcy, who is with a Field Ambulance Tent some eight miles from the neighbourhood in which the "Terriers" were engaged. Tin reverend gentleman writes that Private Briscbe was injured on Friday night and died on Saturday morning. He had been shot through the abdomen, the bullet having passed clean through his pocket Testa- ment. So. serious were his injuries that there was, from the first, practically no chance of a recovery, and, says the Chaplain, though everything possible was done for him, he passed away peacefully on the morning of March 10th. He remained conscious to the end, and asked the Chaplain to write home on his behalf, con- veying his best love to all his relatives arid remembrances to all his friends. He was buried in a peaceful little churchyard hard by the Ambulance Tent.

WOUNDED BELOW THE KNEE.

Private J. Niereer Hit Whilst Trench- Digging.

Private J. Mercer, 27, Ernest-street, off Lovely-lane,- one of the wounded, is a married man and has a family of six children, ranging from a baby of five months up to a thirteen- year-old son.

On Tuesday morning Mrs. Mercer received a. telegram stating that her husband ,had been wounded—a gun-Shot in the leg.

MARKED FOR PROMOTION.

Following the telegrani came the following letter from an officer in Private Mercer's com- pany

March 3rd.

Dear Madam,—l, am writing to tell you that your husband has been wounded. Per- haps you will have' already heard from him. Last night he went off to dig trenches, 70 pr 80 yards to the rear ,of those already occu- pied. The ground which they were digging was supposed to be what is called "dead:" As a matter of fact, there were a gocid many

blown to bits. He also refers to the time he and his comrades from Warrington spent in the trenches, and says: "We had to go into them by night, when it was quite dark. On our way we had to walk over dead Frenchmen and dead horses galore! When we got into the trenches Ave had to put up with the "Jack Johnsons," which rained shells at us, most of them drop- :ping just at the back of the trench and making holes, when they exploded, big enough to put houses in. If one of them had happened to drop into the trenches—well, a good many' of us would have had to be reported missing, or worse."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
"One of the Best Men In My Platoon."

Private Mercer is on of the best men in my platoon and we can ill spare him. He was up for promotion to lance-corporal. He was exceedingly brave and we gave him morphia to ease his pain. I hope he will make a speedy recovery, and,. even if he is sent home, I trust you will find him eager to return to his pals out here. Once more let me ask you not to worry about him. I have told you exactly. what has happened and kept nothing back. Hoping to see him back again in the near future, believe me to be, yours sincerely, BRIAN T. DIXON, 16th Platoon, "D " Com- pany; 4th South Lancashires.

From headquarters on Friday morning, Mrs. Mercer received the following intimation: "I regret to inform you that this day a re-

port 11-as been received from the War Office to the effect that Private Mercer is ill at the General Hospital, Le Treport, suffering from a gun-shot \ wound in the leg."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FIRST CASUALTY.

Private Richard Smith, of the Warrington Territorials, who, as was reported in our last issue, was wounded in action in France on March. Ist, is now in hospital in England, where, it is to be hoped, he may make a rapid and complete recovery. It will be remembered that the gallant private was shot in the back as he was preparing to leave the trenches for a rest, after a spell of severe fighting. His wife, who lives at sso, Cartwright-street, has received a letter from him, in which he says that he has arrived safely at the 'Northern General Military Hospital, Lincoln. He adds that' he arrived in England on Saturday afternoon and that he was then going on all right. With other wounded men, he was landed at Dover and put into a train, and; after a six-hour journey, reached hospital' little the worse for the passage across the Channel and the long ride.

SHOT IN THE SHOULDER.

Lucky H Was No Worse," Writes A

'" Terrier " To His Wife.

Pte. E. Kinsey, 1, Crossley-street, off Brick- street, is an old soldier, who enlisted in the Territorials, although he was 42 years of age and married. The morning before he left Warring- ton for active service an addition was made to his family. Last Saturday his wife was in- formed by letter from her husband that he had been shot in the right shoulder and was in the British General Hospital at Boulogne. I am," he added, "very well at present under the circumstances, so you must not upsek 3-ourself. It is very lucky it was no wocsa Billy Rowlands got knocked over at the same time as me, but he is better off than me, tIF he was only scratched on the left shoulder. Mine is the right! We cannot grumble so ear. I saw a good many old faces after I arrived obt here, and everywhere we went we got a welcome. The The greatest thing we had to con- tend with were the weather conditions."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
A BULLET THROUGH THE ARM.

Nlember Of Fighting Family Makes \ Light Of Ills Hurt.

Three sons, four nephews, and :a brother-in- law, all soldiers! This is the record of the Holmes family, of Scott-street. Of the sons, Corporal Alfred Holmes is a shoe- ing smith with the sth Irish Lancers, and has just returned to the trenches, after having retovered from wounds received at Mons.

Pte. Horace Holmes, fhe next in seniority, is a Maxim gunner in the 4th South Lancashires, nnd Frank, the younger brother, is one of the Warrington " Terriefs," and he is now lying wounded, and writes to his mother from hos- pital. The letter, which she received on Thuri- day morning, ran as follows: "I got a bullet through the Muscles of my right Arm, but I am all right. Don't trouble; it's just my luck, and the last day in the trefiches, too! Then we were to have had a few days' rest. 'I spent my 20th birthday in the trenches, and am wanting the choc ' and • splosh ' (money), as I am abso- lutely 'broke.' . . . I saw our Horace about a week ago. I don't think he has been in the trenches yet. He looks well, and assures me he is 'in the pink.' ,Has our All. come out here again yet? I hear there are some yarns in War- rington about us being cut up, but we have 'done it' on the Germans, and came out with- out a scratch, barring me and one or two others, who received slight wounds. The weather is atill very cold out. here, but shows signs of im- proving. Tell everybody I am all right, but cannot write to them all." The letter was written, on March Bth.

RECRUITING MEETING AT WILDERS- POOL TO-DAY. After .to-day's cup-tie at WildersPool a re- cruiting meeting will be held on the ground, under the chairmanship of Councillor Osborne, when intending recruits will be asked to hand in their names. The Band of the South Lancashire (P.W.V.) Regiment will be in attendance during the Match and will play suitable selections.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
FULL KIT MEN.

Continual Drafts Do Not Diminish the Supply.

CONTENTED SOLDIERS.

BY FRANK KAY.

Private Kay, since sending the article given below, writes to say thk-t he has been selected for "draft" among 150 picked out from 2,000 clerks stationed at his centre; later still he says he has been selected as one of a draft of twenty 'for overseas at any moment." He has been promoted corporal this week. He is to be congratulated upon his early suc- cess, and the best wishes of his colleagues on The Examiner" go with him.

The last link connecting me with civilian life

has now been snapped, for on Wednesday I doffed my own clothes and donned His Majesty's uniform. When I had been here only a few days I was given what is termed a "half kit," which consists of a canvas kit bag, two pairs of boots, service cap, three shirts, three pairs of socks, two pairs of under-pants, a thick cardi- gan vest, one pair of puttees, two towels, razor and shaving brush, hair brush an comb, tooth brush, cap-comforter, and a "housewife." The latter is a handy little outfit containing cotton,. wool, needles, bodkin, thimble, buttons and all the necessaries for mending and stitching. We also received a good rain-coat, which has been of great service to me as well as the others, for we have had a great deal of wet weather. Men who have received the above-named articles are termed " half-kit " men, but although we should not come to much harm with such good and warm clothing on, we were glad when we were paraded for the remainder of our kit, which includes khaki trousers and tunic. When we got our clothing we were paraded in front of one of the sleeping blocks and marched down to the stores. After receiving the articles in an upper room, we marched down to the ground' floor, where a kit number was stamped on all our things for the purpose of identification. Tlze mere fact of wearing khaki makes you feel more of a soldier, and you don't feel so "lost" when you have got the regihiental badge in your cap and the numerals on your epaulettes. The buttons, badge and numerals have to be kept very clean and bright, and there is a general "clean up" at night before getting into bed. Quite a roaring trade is being done down here at Iresent by those who sell the cleaning materials, for all the men—and there are thousands of them—are keen on having their own outfits. •

Fit as a Fiddle. It does not seem six weeks since I left good old Warrington, but still that is the length of time I have been down here, and I feel as "fit as a fiddle." I have almost forgotten what the break from civilian to Army life was like at first, but I. can soon conjure up the vision, when, at meal times, I hear some of the new arrivals talking over their first two days' experiences. We get plenty of exercise and good food, spend most of our time in the open air, sleep well and in comparative comfort, and what could be more conducive to good health? To have answered "the call" will have meant signing a "good health" warrant to many thousands of young men, who, most probably, did not have the opportunities to enjoy the bene- fits of an open-air life before. I have watched with great interest the "finishing off" in drill of a large number ,of men who are now fine specimens of manhood and sready, for anything. Drafts are rapidly being made up and men are being sent away continuously, but still there does not appear to be any great diminution, for England's sons are responding nobly. I have no fear that Warrington will not do its share, for I think the men in the town realise how valu- able their services will be to the country, and will give them willingly. When the war is over and victory is ours, we all hope that War- rington will be amongst the leading towns which have sent the most men in proportion to the population. _

Will, I have not got on a draft to be fully trained and-prepared for the great-task yet, but I am expecting to be picked out within the next few days. The other night we were all told to stand by our beds and not to "turn in." About 10 o'clock the order came "Pack your kits for draft, and parade outside quickly." Clothes, boots and other articles were hurriedly pushed into the bags, and we rushed outside, only to be told that we were not wanted that night. The same thing may happen any night, and I only hope our turn will come soon. We stand a better chance now that we have our uniforms, for preference is always given to " full kit" men. It is a fine sight ,to see the men on church parade, the Scottish regiments preceded by the pipers and drummers and the other regiments by their respective bands. After -the service

the men parade on one or other of the large squares, are put through several movements, and then marched back to barracks. They are watched and admired by large crowds of people, and it really is a post inspiring sight. It makes one feel that these men of such fine physique will make a great impression when they get at the enemy. The "Scotties ", seem especially anxious to get "out there," and they seem quite optimistic when they ask the question, "Did you ever know anyone to sit on a thistle?" A loud laugh is the only answer, and this is renewed when one man suggests that the Kaiser should try it with a kilt on. Best Night of the Week.

Now that we are in uniform we have to salute all officers—lieutenants and those of higher rank—and they have to respond. In a large bar- racks town like this there are numerous officers, and one has to salute pretty often. If a private or N.C.O. is caught passing an officer without saluting he is liable to be sent to the guard- room. When I tell you that for every 24 hours a man is in the guardroom he has 48 hours' pay stopped, you can rest assured that we have an officer "spotted ," as soon as possible, salute and pass on, resting contented knowing that we do not run the risk of the " G.R." The mention of pay reminds me that I have not described how we receive "the necessary" every week. On our "best night of the week," we parade outside one of the blocks, and as the names are called out in alphabetical order we line up ready to go in before the paymaster. When all the names are called, the " A's " lead off, and the boys strike up with the very appropriate Morns "See them shuffle along," as gradually we draw nearer to the entr,nce to the block. Once in the room silence has to be observed, for as each man comes up his name is called out by a cor- poral to an officer at the pay table. The latter refers to the pay-book and shouts out the amount opposite the soldier's name, and the pay- master puts the money on the table. "Tommy" goes to the table, salutes smartly with the right hand, picks his money up with the left hand, salutes again, turns about smartly with a smil- ing face, walk; out of the block, and generally into one of the "homes" where he is soon eating sweetcakes and drinking his tea, coffee or cocoa. On occasions we have to wait a long time, but of all the waits we have, this is the one we mind least Of all, for there is something tangible at the end of it. Some of the men who have been detailed for "fatigue" work or guard duty earlier in the day cannot get their pay unless they go down to the offices on the followinx da:r and explain why they were absent when their names were called out. Pay night is really a "joy night," for then " Tommy " can get those little delicacies such as jam tarts, currant cake, jellies and other sweetmeats, of which, I have observed, he is so fond. When it is drawing near to B.M or 8.45 he makes off for his sleeping quarters, where he inquires of his pals if they have been paid, and if so, how much did they get, for some weeks a man may get 2s. or 3s. short, but the amount is made up, however, on some future pay night, and he is considered very wealthy who has drawn a lot of arrears, although his chums seem to forget the fact that he may have been paid short for two. or three, weeks. For my part I may say that I have had my money "just right" each week, and indeed" it is better coming that way than spasmodically. If a man runs short of money during the week, which happens very often, he is kept in cigar- ettes and stamps for letters home by one or other of his more fortunate pals. There is nothing stingy or mean about "Tommy," and he shares out with a cheerfulness that does one, good to see. He is a jolly good fellow all through, and those who have not done so should go to tie nearest recruiting off/ce and come in. contact with the genuine article, amidst the barracks and parade grounds.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

" Turn Again, W . . . ton." The mettjesome Councillor Pemberton some- what contemptuously contrasts the average Warrington ratepayer with the wenn whielli turns when one treads on it. Apathetic.,l supine, torpid, phlegmatic, pachydermatous,. long-suffering, easy-going, iambi:lire—so &evidenttity does the desperately-in-earnest councilor alter- nately, impatiently and not altogether justly regard the constituency; and comfortable house- holders smile tolerantly at their .excited 'critic. and remain unmoved and apparently indiffer- ent. Alas ! this inertness and :seeming &insensi- bility on the part of the multitude who pay the piper but make no great &outward show •of concern as to tune is by no means peculiar to Warrington. The scandalised Councillor Pem- berton deplores the general &unconcern 'of the local burgesses in relation to municipal tiff airs as singular and extraordinary, and declares that in the whole course of !his life the 'has not elsewhere experienced conditions at all com- parable with the state of &likings in this bor- ough. It does not appear to have occurred to the 'doughty councillor as a possible explana- tion that, having deliberatelrelrosen! their repre- sentatives, the electors have not lost confidence in them, and are content lo :leave to their better judgment the determination of questions respecting which they, outside the Council Chamber are of necessityeomparatively ill-in- formed upon details. Increased Rates.

Having 'read how The Budget raising War- rington's rates from Ss. in the £ to 9s. 4d. in the 2 had been adopted after. characteristic de- bate, I turned up the third annual White Paper (unfortunately the latest publication of the kind in my possession) showing the pretty recent state of the finance of local authorities in general. It gives partimilars of expenditure amounting to £146,000,000, and a loan debt of wellnigh .2414,000,M, and, in view of what was said during last weekls 'discussion here, it is specially noteworthy that education constitutes far and away the heaviest item of expenditure, viz., .230,500,000. It is further remarkable that well under one-third of the money raised by loan in the year under review was on account of municipal trading enterprises—which, by-the- bye, on the whole, is shown to be distinctly pro- fitable, certain departments amply compensat, jug others upon which there happens to be a loss. A Reminder.

Warringtonians may, moreover, just be reminded that there are some classes of public works that should not be expected to be directly profitable. I have never before lived in a dis- trict.where, for example, there was any idea of profit-making out of Corporation waterworks. These, as other writers on the subject have. taken pains to point out, are meant to ensure a plentiful and steady supply of water at the lowest rate possible, and plenty and steadiness are always put before any considerations of cost and profit. It has been further argued, reason- ably enough, that in any large view municipal water enterprises would, like sanitation or edu- cation, be classed as necessary governmental functions, not as trading undertakings. The strictly trading activities of the local authori- ties, like gas, trams and electricity (the reader does not forget That I am still dealing with the country as a whole), are thus much more profit- able than the lumping of the figures together indicates. Nor ought it to be necessary to add that the figures at the best do not represent anything like the full benefit conferred by undertakings which have abolished private monopoly on commodities ,of 'general consump- tion and greatly reduced the charges for them.

No Comparison. May I suggest that to affect to compare in an ordinary way municipal and private enterprise is futile and puerile? Anyhow, it is calculated seriously to mislead the unwary. Everybody should know that, unlike a private .company, a Corporation like that op Warrington, has not only to pay interest on its borrowed money, but to provide a sinking fund for the gradual liquidation of the debt. Besides, while a big dividend is the recognised measure of the suc- cess and stability of an ordinary trading com- pany financed by share and loanholders, it is rightly claimed that the aim of the average cor- poration is to give the maximum of benefits and convenience to the citizens; hence, at certain hours on some sections, the tram service is main- tained at a monetary sacrifice in order that workers may enjoy healthy residential condi- tions away from their daily industrial environ- ment. One might also make comparison as to wages and hours of labour in the case of a model public body; but I have already been tempted farther afield than I intended at the outset, and I merely invite the thoughtful and intelligent ratepayer (and, believe me, Jeremiah, his name is ldgion) to continue the investigation at his leisure, and, in endeavouring to arrive at a fair working estimate of the real value to the com- munity of the Corporation trading departments as a whole, to give due weight to the supremely important and essential considerations which I have indicated.

Music Indispensable. While the Warrington Town Council were solemnly discussing the desirability of dispens- ing with the open-air concerts for next summer, a speaker at the Wycliffe 3femorial Hall voiced the sentiments, not only of his numerous hearers, but doubtless of the great majority of the people of these parts, in submitting that a bit of music is an excellent thing in these times. I know a minister who is also a Mus. Bac., and he strongly holds that there is actually some use in music as medicine—that it can make one forget pain. Well, as to that, Shakespeare has it in "Romeo and Juliet"—

When griping grief the heart doth wound, And doleful dumps the mind depress, Then music with his silver sound With speedy help doth send redress. Striking Expert Testimony.

QUAKERS AND THE WAR.

Sir,—l am invariably interested in the " Jot- ctings of an Old Journalist," and his last contri- ,bution was no exception when dealing with 'Quakerism and war. In the opening passages of that section'he tells us that Quaker views on war is New TeStament teaching. In fact, "it is the ideal of Christians of all denominations." But, alas! it is an ideal only. Now at tne close of his remarks in dealing with the present war, he says, "I, for one, rejoice in the thought that our own country is destined to play a prominent and noble part in the supreme and necessary and (as I hold) essentially Chris- tian work." Mark you, "essentially Chris- tian work." The ideal has suddenly become the actual! Yet somehow to me there is a strange antithesis. Perhaps your correspond- ent will be good enough to explain the recon- ciliation.

HAROLD GRAY.

KNUTSFORD LIBERALS.

War Activities That Supplanted Party Politics.

The report of the Knutsford Division Liberal Association, passed at the recent annual meet- ing, stated that the transfer of the Central Office from Knutsford to Manchester had enabled officers and members to keep in touch better with the Secretary. Owing to the war, only seven months of political work were necessary. "During the spring months,". the report continues, "a large number of meetings were held to explain the land and housing pro- posals Of the Government, and later an open- air campaign was commenced, but could of neces.sity pot be continued beyond July. Mr. P. M. Oliver addressed practically all the meetings, and a keen interest was manifested' in the proposed reforms which must now be postponed to, let us hope, a not too distant future. Every voter in the division received a copy of the booklet published by the Central Land and ousing Council. This was effected by the excellent assistance of our District Com- mittees, who undertook and carried out the addressing, sorting and delivering of upwards of ten thousand copies. "Though at one time it had seemed that an honourable peace might have been maintained, the early days of August showed that this hope was in vain. Loyalty to herself, to her best traditions, and to her treaty obligations made abstention from the conflict ion the part of 'Great Britain impossible. Having entered into the struggle, she will pursue it to the end in vindication Of the 'rights of Belgium, for the maintenance of international good faith and the possibility of human progress. "Under these circumstances your committee felt that the organisation should, as far as possible, assist the Government in its work of creating a new Army. Many of your officials and members co-operated with their political opponents in promoting meetings to explain the incidents leading 'up to the war and to encour- age enlistment, and in other ways. . "A number are serving their country in fhe Army, amongst whom we may mention Capt. J. H. Whitworth, our former'candidate; Capt. H. G. Roberts, of Stockton Heath; and Mr. Mark Fletcher, of Knutsford. "In the Wilmslow district, where it was found that no Recruiting Committee existed, a joint Political Committee was formed, with the Liberal. and Conservative agents as joint hon.- secretaries. Mr. Cliffe has also neen actively engaged on the executives of the Knuts- ford War Relief and Belgian Committees"

it is likewise capable of expressing definite emo- tion? For the definite character of an emotion rests entirely on the meaning involved in it." This is rather a hard saying, and involves much more than I have space for. To hasten on, Dogiel, a_Russian, finds that: "(1) Music exerts an influence on the circulation of the blood„ (2) The blood pressure sometimes rises, sometimes falls. (3) The action of musical tones and pipes on animals and men expresses itself for the most part by increased frequency of the beats of the heart. (4) The variations in the circulation consequent upon musical sounds coincide with changes in the breathing, though they,may also be observed quite independently of it (5). The variations in the blood pressure are dependent on the pitch and loudness of the sound and on the tone colour. (6) In the varia- tions of the blood pressure the peculiarities of the individual, whether men or lower animals, are plainly apparent; and even nationality in the case of man has some effect." In a pam- phlet,." The Therapeutics of Music," Dr. Lilley states that "music affects the nerves which govern the supply of blood. By blood supply is nutriton effected. Therefore to improve nutri- tion, hnd consequently health itself, it is im- portant that the stimuli employed should be of a character favourable to this end. And what is there more universally attractive and bene- ficial than healthy musical, ithfluence?" For this and a great deal More in amplification and support, I am indebted to our musical parson aforesaid. - • -

What the Lady of the Lamp Found. I may only add the following decidedly per- tinent letter by Florence Nightingale, one of the immortals, who was lately the subjept of a memorable local lecture:—

"The effect of mirage upon the sick has been scarcely at all noticed. In fact its expensive- ness, as it is now, makes any general applica- tion of it out of the question. I will only re- mark here, that wind instruments, including the human voice, and stringed instruments, capable of continuous sound, have generally a beneficial 'effect; while the pianoforte, with such instruments that have no continuity of sound, have just the reVerse. The finest piano playing will damage the sick, while an air like 'Home, Sweet Home' on the most ordinary' grinding organ, will sensibly soothe them, and this is quite independent of association." "Wind instruments," be it especially noted, "have generally a beneficial effect." Who says, now, that we ought not next season to have our bands in the Park as usual?

In the same period thir- teen frinerals were conducted at the church. Six of the funerals were those of persons who had passed "tlie allotted span," whilst two were those of persons who were verging on 70 years of age. The average age at de'ath of those interred in the churchyard during the two months was over 60 years.

Result of "Seeing Soldiers Off."—Eustace Bennett, of Gaskell-street, Stockton Heath, wis charged •at the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday with having been drunk and disorderly on Sunday. He admitted being drunk, but denied being disorderly.—P.C. Tudor said that at 9.45 on Sunday night he saw Bennett leave the tramcar, drunk, and walk down the pas- sage leading to Gaskell-street. Presently re- ceiving complaints, witness followed, and heard defendant talking very loudly. He also found that Bennett had been acting impro- perly. The man refused to go home, and said he would kill four of his (witness's) sort.—De- fendant said he had been to see a few pals off to the Front, and had had a drop too much drink. He was innocent of any disorder, and was just about to open his own door when the constable stopped him.—Superintendent Owen said defendant had been eight times before the Court previously, and on the last occasion made a similar excuse for drunkenness.—A fine of ss. and costs, or fourteen days, was imposed.. .

The :proceeds, which were in aid of the-church funds, totalled £3 10s.

TENDED 147 PATIENTS.

District Nurse's Splendid Work

Among the Sick Poor.

The annual meeting of the Stockton Heath and District Nursing Association was held on Monday evening in the Parish Room. Mr. Vere B. Davies tpresident) occupied the chair.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
Nurse's Busy Twelve Months.

Mr. W. H. Woodcock (the secretary and treasurer) read. the ,annual reports. That of the nurse showed that during the year she had nursed 147 cases, 48 of which were medical cases and 91 surgical cases. Of the patients 104 were either convalescent or had been cured, 9 were transferred to hospital, 25 died, and 8 remained on the books. The total number of visits .paid to patients was 2,474. The Secretary's report on the work done showed that the nurse's work had been very helpful. He hoped that despite the very many claims made upon them at present contributors wpuld not forget the fund for the village nurse. The year began with a balance in hand of ,t 63 16s. 7d. and ended with one of £55 3s. 2d. No special cases had been dealt with, and there- fore the Coronation Fund had not been drawn upon and remained at £l6 9s.- 4d. The total expenditure for the year had been £lO4 Is. 7d. The Chairman remarked that the small at- tendance was a sign, that satisfaction was being given.

Society's Position Quite Satisfactory. In reviewing the work of the society, the Secretary observed that, so far as the finances were concerned, they fould consider things quite satisfactory. They would observe that although the expenses were quite normal ,the balance had gone down 8. The!deficiericy was rather less than it was last year and was due to the fact that the income was not, quite so large. That was not, however, the time to Make a special appeal, but they had to bear in mind that something like the present amount received in subscriptions must be kept up. People would realise how valuable the services of a nurse were in the homes of the floor. He did not think there was need of any special %appeal to the public. They could no on very nicely for two or three years yet, and, no doubt, the Ladies' Committee, irquld do the same work as before in looking after the sub- scriptions. - -

DROWNED IN DANE.

Shawl Found Ott Canal [lznik': The

Inquest.

Mrs. Sarah Ellen Brown (58), of Canal-bank, (by Lymm Bridge) Vas found drowned on Wednesday.

For six weeks she had been suffering from influenza, and had become greatly depressed. Till a fortnight ago she was 'attended by Dr. De Jong tinder the care of her niece, Miss Rebecca Bellis, she was satisf.actorily progres- sing, and on Tuesday evening Miss Bellis went out for a few minutes to the post. Upon her return her aunt was missing. Promptly Miss Bellis raised the -alarm,, and all night various friends searched for her—but without 'success.

Her shawl, however, was 'found on thc canal bank. •

The Body Found.

Early the following morning the search was resumed, the police conducting the vrappling of the •canal, with the: assistance of several other men. An hour after noon their worst feamwere realised, for after flushing the Dane they found, the body in the water behind Dane House the residence of Mr. A. Watkin). The body was got out by ,Mr. Harry Broom and conveyed home. _

The inquest was conducted* on Thursday afternoon, at the, Police Station, by Mr. A. E. White (deputy-coroner); evidence being given by George Brown, waterman, husband of de- ceased; Rebecca Bellis, Harry Broom and P.C. Atherton.

The verdict was "Suicide whilst temporarily insane."

The funeral takes place this afternoon at the Parish Church.
