SOLDIER-HEADMASTER.

Presentation To His Deptkty.

Mr. W. Hawkins, head master of the Lymm Grammar School, returned yesterday to his duties. These he left at the outbreak of war, being "Called up" as captain of the Terri- torial Engineers. Having trained his men in Cornwall since that time, he is able to resume peace duties (on Monday) owing to their de- mobilisation.

In the meantime, however, the duties of tile head mastership have been efficiently carried out, and the standard and attendances of the school fully maintained, by his deputy, Mr. C. C. Parez. As Mr. Parez now relinquishes the head mastership, the staff and scholars yester- day afternoon took the opportunity of present- ing him • with ti beautiful silver cigarette ease, and 'many highly-complimentary tributes were paid to the recipient, who voiced his deep ap- preciation and thanks in an appropriate reply.

TO COTTAGE GARDENERS.

L3mm Lady's War-time Offer.

Our advertisement columns give details of a Garden- Competition ,originated by -Mrs. John- son, of The Old Tannery, 'Cherry-lane, Lymm. Prizes will be given:, flSr the best vegetables, grown entirely by the owner, in L3-min parish cottage-gardens or allotments. , The competition is run from purely philan- thropic motives. Mrs. Johnson informs us that she got the idea from the " Way Mail"— which is offering gardening prizes at a show to be held some 'time in September. The judges for the Lymm competition will inspect the gardens early in September, in order that those who should desire to exhibit in the "Daily Mail" competition may do So. Outside Gentleman to Judge.

Mrs. Johnson is herself an amateur gardener of great experience, and will of course assist in the judging. She has, however, deciaed that the main part of the task shall be: performed by an "outside" gentleman, in order to avoid the possibility of a suggestion of favouritism or local influence.

The Urban District Council, it may. be noted, :neither owns nor .controls public allotments or gardens of any sort, such aS° are owned or con- trolled by the Warrington Corporation. There- fore, all the more credit is due to the lady who has originated the excellent idea, and who proviaes the prizes. Mrs. Johnson must re 7 ceive names .of intending competitors before April 30th.

Wesley Guild.—What might be 'very appro- priately called a "Tennyson evening was held in the schoolroom on Monday evening, and was presided over by Mrs. Wheatley, of Latchford, who, in her opening address, explained the object of the gathering, which was to raise money for the Trust funds. THE CHEAPEST PASTIME.

Of all the popular pastimes of the moment there is no question about it that cycling comes easily first in the point of cheapness. With a good machine, after the first cost, there is practically no other expenditure. A Harrin- gay cyclist, Mr. R. W. Priest, of 9, Terront- road, has been keeping a log of his riding since last mid-summer, when he bought a second- hand Raleigh. Since .then he .has used it every day, except Sundays, On an 'average of 15 miles a day, riding through rain, fog, snow,; and everything that came along. Here is his log for the period

Miles. Daily rides, 216 days at 15 miles 3,240 Club runs, 22 at 30 miles aVerage 660 Brighton and back and tour l3O Southend and back, twice

Total 4,195 In all,this he has not spent a penny on the bicycle excepting ss. 6d. for a new tyre cover at the end of 4,000 miles, and the machine is running to-day as well as at the start, so that his capital; as represented by it, is still intact. Of the ease and pleasure of the pastime top much cannot be said. In the cage mentioned Mr. Priest recounts bow he rode up Muswell Hill, one of the, steePest round London, with a fully-loaded machine, while the seven miles to business occupies him but 20 to 25 minutes— much quicker than he could cover it by 'bus or train. What must be the saving in fares alone?

IMPORTANCE OF CLEANLINESS.

Questions and correspondence should be ad- dressed to "The Hub," c/o The Editor. Re- plies cannot be sent by post, and all queries will be answered in our columns.

CHEAP VERSUS DEAR CYCLES.

A question which often greatly perplexes those who are taking up cycling is whether a cheap machine is reliable, seeing that there is so big a difference in the price of such a mount and the "do luxo " model. The answer is un- doubtedly in the affirmative, if a good firm is selected. In all models turned out from a firm of standing, the body of the machine, that is the tubing and the brazing, is the same in all respects. The higher price charged for the more expensive machine is due to what may be termed the finish. The high grade cycle scores in the excellence of plating and enamelling, a thing which does prolong the life of the machine and , adds to its appearance.. Moreover, first quality cycles are fitted with the best tyres pro- curable, and the same may be said as regards saddles, brakes, pedals, lamps, &c. The uniniti- ated need not be afraid to purchase a -Cheap model from a reliable firm, but all the same he must not expect to get an article just as good as regards the superficial refinements. It would, in fact, be unreasonable to do so. - •

===== 0002601_19150313_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
TO KEEP THE LAW.

The two leading cycling organisations have recently had much abuse hurled at their heads by a certain section of the motoring public on account of their action in publishing a circular drawing attention to the recent rear lighting order. The two organisations have been guilty of but one thing. They have advised their mem- bers to adhere to the law, but, at the same time, they have pointed out the fact that the order is only temporary in nature, and that its applica- tion is local. Motorists have come to the con- clusion that it would be better all round—but especially for themselves—if the law were made permanent and universal; hewe they are exas- perated that cyclists should look forward to the future when the special need for rear hats have disappeared. 'I think it stands to reason that cyclists will do no more than obey the laws, but as a body they are faithfully carrying out the regulations. Let motorists do all they can legitimately to get the order made permanent, but we reserve the right to fight against such proceeding to the best of our ability.- It seems unfair that the two organisations should be abused for the issue of the circular.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
THOSE WHO BREAK THE LAW.

The section of the motoring public to which I have just referred is, fortunately, only a com- paratively small one. Those, however, who have waxed most indignant with cyclists are the very ones about whom much has been written in the past. It is they who always seem to glory when they break the law themselves. tet we find them to-day telling the cyclist that he must not only obey the law, but also that he must not think about the temporary and locol nature of the new order. The legal speed limit is. broken every time such motorists take out their cars; if they can outwit the police in any way at all for days afterwards they brag about their cleverness. If they succeed in making the order permanent we will continue to obey. It is a surprising fact, but the idea still appears to be prevalent, that the law is for the masses and

ALEIGH CYCLE Co. Nottinsha

not for the classes. The greab. majority of cyclists belong to the former—the term " impe- cunious cyclists" has been given them before to-day—therefore it is only right that the dis- orderly motorist should endeavour to keep the orderly cyclists in the narrow path of rectitude.

THE MOTOR CYCLE VALET.

A number of firms in different parts of the country make a speciality of valetting motor, cars. By means of a quarterly inspection they guarantee to keep the car in good running order. I have just come across a firm in. the Midlands which includes motor cycles in its scheme. There are a number of riders who find it diffi- cult to devote the necessary amount of time to attend to their machines, as such a delicate piece of mechanism deserves. Of course, I urn not referring to the annual overhaul, which is generally entrusted to the professional mechanic. but to the small adjustments that are required from time to time. The firm referred to sends out a man fully equipped to do all the needed repairs. All that is necessary is attended to, such as decarbonising the engine, toning the valves, adjusting the brakes, clutch or magneto. The rate of subscription in the case of cars varies from £5 ss. to £lO 10s. per annum; in tho case of • motor cycles from £1 11s. Gd. to .0 3se All renewals have to be paid for in addition, but, that is only reasonable.

"MATTER IN THE WRONG PLACE."

This is the definition given, by scientists' to., what the man in the street applies the terni:' “dirt." Scientifically, the former is the more correct, but, all the ,same, matter out of place Is a nuisance.,., Dirt. is, a, conlfant source of trouble to the iiidtdriSt;ifiUrif irl,r6lrtirro ttrer-

spirit, oil or water system of the machine, it• acts as a deterrent to consistent running. For this reason everything should be strained before filling up. The most sensitive of the three iP the petrol supply, since even a minute speck of dirt may choke the jet, with the result that all power is lost. The great majority of petrol tanks have a gauze fitted at the inlet, and a number of carburetters. are• also fitted in the same way. A funnel, without a strainer, should never be used. For this purpose one of the best materials I have used is wash leather. It will keep back everything except the spirit. The oil pipes are of a small bore and dirty oil is bound to clog these up sooner or later. Again. the same may happen with the water pipes and the radiator. Hence the advantage of strain- ing both oil and wiater. •

===== 0002601_19150313_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
A GREASY SUBJECT.

I wonder when we cyclists are going to have-a really good oilcan. Perhaps I have been un- usually unfortunate, but after trying various makers and patterns. lam still convinced that perfection in this direction is far from accom- plished. What can be more annoying than, a leaky oilcan? If it happens to be in the tool bag behind the saddle, something always seemo to disturb it, and the next time the unfortunate rider has to mend a puncture or effect an adjust- ment, be finds everything covered with oil, while the bag itself, if not actually ruined, is in a decidedly unpleasant condition. Oil can-Er-fit- ting into the saddle pillar are better only in their more limited opportunities for damage, and are 'usually liable to escape on to the road, and consequently be lost altogether. Very often, of course, ao leak can be eaSily remedied, a new washer being all that is required. This can be made by cutting a piece of leather to the neces- sary size and making an opening for the nozzle to pass through, then screwing this up very tightly. Another point to remember is-liefer to fill an oil, can to its utmost capacity,lor this is another very prolific cause of leakage.

TO-DAY'S CUP-TIE.

Will AVarrington Atiajn Beat Salford?

If the spring-like weather -which marked yesterday centinues over to-day the Warring- ten-Salford cup-tie should see a record gate at This meeting, which is in the second round of the Northern Union Cup Competition. will be the fourth between Warrington and the representatives of the Royal Borough this sea- son, all three previous encounters having been won by Warrington, and it is confidently anti- cipated that they will again come out on top to-day. The scores in the _previous matches were: 5--0 (at Wilderspool), 22-10 (at Wil- derspool, in the Lancashire Cup), and 11-0 (at Weaste). It must be borne in mind, however, that in the game at Weaste Salford were but weakly represented, the first team being under suspension, with the result that they had to put their second string in the field, with the exception of one first team man, who had escaped suspension. Warrington are fielding a strong side and it will be noticed with interest that it includes the gallant artilleryman, Chesters, now pro- moted to the rank of Bombardier, and whose recent appearance with his old team was such a. signal success. There are other changes in the team, as compared with that which did duty on Saturday. Brookes plays centre, in place of lligham, and .Mather and Fairhurst are dropped from the forward line to make room for Chesters and Green. The teams selected for the match are as follows, it being noticed that Salford have picked fifteen men, two of Whom, of course, will stand down :

Warrington. 1 Jolley. 2 Taberner, 3 Tranter, 4 Brookes (captain),

5 Bradshaw. 6 Dainteth, 7 Young. 8 Cox, 9 Fearnley, 10 Green, 11 W. Cunliffe 12 T. Cunliffe, 13 Bombardier Chesters.

S E. J.. Thomas, 10 Rees, 14 Currie, 12 Bevan, 13 Goldsmith, 11 Woods, 9 Wilkinson. 15 W. Waite, 6. E. C. May, 7 John. 2 Callender, 3 W. Thomas (captain), 4 Love- luck, 5 Mesley. 1 Launce. Salford. The officials selected for the match are:— Referee, Mr. J. F. May (St. Helens); touch- judges, Messrs. H. Heath (Runcorn) and H. Ball (Wigan).

A RULE ALTERED

A representative of the Vulcan Bowling Club applied, for that club's admission to the asso- ciittion! There were, he said, a considerable number of Warrington men employed there, but they were prevented from taking part in the competitions, because a rule stated that the radius for membership was two miles from the borough boundary. If that rule was altered to read "three miles from the borough boun- dary" they wou,ld be within the limit.

_ The rule was altered to admit the Vulcan Club to membership. It was decided again to affiliate with the Lancashire County Association. OBJECTION TO PROFESSIONALS.

Mr. Beswick made A strong protest against professionals playing in their—amateur—compe- titions, and alleged that the committee were aware that professionals were playing in the association's handicap, works and merit com- petitions. The association did nothing to pre- vent it, and it was not right. After mention- ing the name of a player whom he alleged to be a professional, and who had taken part in their competitions, Mr. Bcswick asked if that player was allowed to compete with them.? If they were going to be affiliated with the Amateur Association, then let that association look after them.

The Chairman said there was something in Mr. Beswiek's statement.

Mr. Jennings: The conimittee cannot object to a man until they have an objection laid 'be- fore Ahem in writing. • (Hear, -hear.) The Secretary pointed out that the County Association passed the name of that particular player. If there was a complaint, he left. it to the. County Association. The objection should be made privately, and no one need know by 'whom it had been made. It was 'probable, added Mr. Patrick, that the association would not recognise that player next time. Mr. Beswiek said be was not satisfied. He was not opposed to any man personally, but he objected to the principle of professionals in an amateur association.

500 MEMBERS "JOIN UP."

There was a good attendance at the Horse and Jockey Inn, Winwick-road, on Monday evening—when (in the absence of the Associa- tion Chairman, Mr. J. Willett, who had sus- tained an accident at work) Mr. F. Dean pre- sided over the annual meeting of the Warring- ton Works' Amateur Bowling Association. Despite the war, a successful year's work was reported by the Secretary and Treasurer.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
MAJOR CROSFIELD WRITES FROM FRANCE.

Mr. John Willett shaving been re-appointed delegate to the British Crown Green Associa- tion, Mr. W. Patrick (secretary) statecf- that the question of the presidency had been consid: ered by the committee, and he had written to Major G. R. Crosfield pointing out the position of the association and inviting him to accept re-election if possible. The Major had replied from France:

"I much appreciate the _wish_ of your corn-

mitee, and shall be very pleased to accede to it. I am glad to hear that you are lying low with your bowling programme this year, which I think'is very right and proper under the circumstances. Thanks very much for your good wishes. We have a good lot- of fellows here, and hope to give a very good account of ourselves." (Applause.) Major Crosfield was unanimously re-elected president. TROUBLE WITH THE GREEN.

"It is with very, great pleasure," said Mr. Patrick, "that I have to report another success- ful season—in spite of some of the most serious drawbacks an association of this description could be called upon to face.

"The first drawback was the state of the green. It looked at the back-end as if a little re-laying would do no harm, and those reSpon- Bible decided to re-lay certain portions during the close season. Unfortunately the green did not pick up as quickly as was expected, and we were saddled with a green hardly fit to play upon at the commencement of the season. But it improved as the season went on, and we are expecting to have it in good condition for this season.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
Strikes and the Competitions.

"Just as the season opened we had one of the ;works in town engaged in a strike, which was the cause of at least two teams-not entering the Finlayson ' Cup Competition. In spite of this the competition had a record entry of 72 teams of twelve a-side, representing 39 works. • The winners were the Bewsey Wire Ropery, who defeated the Assurance Agents in the final. One interesting item of the competition was the very close results of some of the matches—which speaks well for the handicapping. Seventy- three matches were 'played. Of these, three ;were drawn, one won by 1 point, five by 2, three by 3, four by 4, four by 5, one by six, two by 7, one by 8, and five by 9. Twenty-three matches were won by between 10 and 24 points, and 52 out of the 73 matches by 24 points or under. This, lam sure, is a good Average; but it is more interesting still that to complete these 73 matches 756 individual games were played, and the average 'win was less than two points! • .

"The Cup Competition over, we entered upon our first Individual Merit Competition, and we have to thank our President (Major Crosfield) for providing the handsome shield and also for giving the first prize. (Hear, hear.) But just as we commenced taking entries the Tanners' dispute arose, and as we had nine tanneries in our competition you will at once see its effect. However, 44 entered; and the competition was won by A. Weave (of Fleteher's)—the runner- up being J. Jolley (Corporation).

===== 0002601_19150313_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
War! 500 Members Enlist

"We thonSet about our annual handicap, ex- pecting to have a huge entry. But hardly had we commenced to take entries when our coun- try was thrown into the greatest conflict ever known, and the appeal made by His Majesty the King and his- Ministers for soldiers was such that some 500 of our membeirs gave up their sport and enlisted. Consequently our entries for the handicap fell from 660 in 1913 to 472 in 1914. Sixty-four prizes were provided, Lady Greenall once again showing her great generosity to our -association, by •giving the first prize of .25 5s.— for which we are very. thankful. This was won by P. Wright (of Crosfield's), the runner-up being G. Cook (of the Ropery team). The re- mainder of the prizes went to the workpeoplo of :,,Rylands', 9; Pearson and Knowles, 9; White- cross, 6; Cheshire Lines, 6; Plumbers and Pain- ters, 5; Crosfield's, 9; Carpenters and Joiners, 3; Longford Wire, 3; Naylor's, 3; Wire Ropery; 2; Greening's, 2; Walker's, 2; Monks, Hall, 2; County Asylum, 2; Warrington Tannery, 1; Assurance Agents, 1; Fletcher's, 1; London and North-Western Railway, 1; 'and Firth, 1. At the completion Lady Greenall presented the prizes to the successful competitors.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
" Better Lie Low."

"In conclusion, whilst we are expecting tO go on with our sport during this season, we can- not promise anything great in prizes—as we have done in the past. We feel with our Presi- dent that we had better' 'lie low' whilst our country is in need of more men, and not do anything that will have a tendency to keep men at home. We appeal to all the bowlers to assist us during, the coming season. We hope to run various competitions to raise money for the various funds in town foil our soldiers and their families, and we appeal to those who, through age, infirmity or work, are unable to fight for their King and country, to do the next best think—to see to it that those left behind by our soldiers do not want." (Hear, hear, and applause.) The Secretary's. report was adopted nem. con.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
INCREASED RESOURCES.

Mr. A. E. Burdett, the hon. treasurer, pre- sented the balance sheet. The association, he said, comMenced the season with a ,balance at the bank of £1 Os. 3d. The auditors, in their 1913 report, bad suggested that for such a large association the balance in the bank was totally inadequate. This suggestion had been acted upon by the committee, who had husbanded their resources to some extent and were able to show a handsome balance at the bank of .00 4s. 7d. (Applause.) Of that amount 26 19s. 10d. belonged to a special reserve fund which ;would be devoted to providing premises of their own at a very early date. The sum would not be used for any other purpose, although the provision of the premises was at present post-, poned.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
Prizes and Charities.

Some of them would perhaps wonder why the total for the prizes did not come up to that for the previous year. They must remember that the entries for the season were nowhere as high as those for 1913, and that now this terrible conflict had been forced upon them their sub- scription list would not receiVe'the same en- couragement as hitherto, because there were so many calls—for very worthy objects—being made upon the people's purses. :Therefore they felt bound to husband their resources to some extent—and they had not had a single complaint about the prize. (Hear, hear.) The charity match had also been the means of raising a little money, and, along with the Warrington Caledonian Association, they had contributed £l5 each to the Poor Children's Camp and the Nursing Association. (Applause.) The committee, instead of tnis time participat-# ing in their annual picnic, decided to abandon it, and handed the amount which was due to them, along, with a sum granted by the asso- ciation, to the local Territorial Equipment Fund. (Hear, hear.) Several of their vice- presidents and other subscribers looked upon this as a very handsome manner in which to deal with sport and charity. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

AN EXCITING DRAW AT LONGFORD.

Notes by " Linesman."

On Saturday the chief interest in local foot- ball was centred in the second round of th.?. Starkey Cup Competition, when three games were set down for decision, the most specula- tive being the meeting of the Orford White Star and Fairfield United, as they are running a neck-and-neck race for the League champion- ship. In their League games both have won on their own ground. This game in the Cup ties was looked upon by the respective teams and supporters as the rubber contest. The White Star had the choice of ground, but owing to the rain during the week, it was well known that their ground would scarcely be fit to play such an important match upon, and at the _last moment they agreed to play on the United ground. A good number of spectators turned up, despite the unpleasant condition of the morning. A streng drying wind greatly interfered with the play. From the start it was evident that the White Star were on their mettle, and were out -to win. Such good forni did they show that when the interval arrived they held a four clear goals' lead. This they further increased in the second portion, and when the referee blew the final whistle the United were completely Nailed by their rivals. A defeat of six goals to none was a surprise to everybody.

Longford United were favoured by choice of ground in their tie with Hollins Green. Neither club was fully represented. Sturgess, the home goalkeeper, failed to appear, and Wainwright, a new player .to the United, was not eligible to play. Owing to the good reputation of the visitors, many doubtful opinions were expressed as to the ability of the home. team winning. The visitors were minus the services of Mellor, their sterling centre-half, owing to injuries, and it was chiefly in the middle line that the weakness of the visitors was most evident. The Greenites first had the advantage of the wind, and held the lead at the interval, but when the United had he wind in their favour they quickly made the scores level. Before the close they scored two more goal § and eventually- ran out winners by three ,goals to one. -

The tie between Paddington and Earlestown Mission was, a keenly contested game, and ended in a draw, each team having one goal to their credit. There was plenty of 'evidence shown that the Paddingtonians' victors- of the previous • week over the White Star had given the players fresh confidence in themselves, and so well did they Play .that the visitors, who were expecting to win rather comfortably, were extended to the full, and at the finish Were very well satisfied to come away with a draw, which will give them . the opportunity of try- ing again to get into the semi-final.•

The match Walton v., Earlestown ,in the:" Starkey" Cup Competition, has been awarded to the Earlestown Club. on the ground that Walton has not complied With _certain rules. .

break through on his own, but was driven back. Up to this stage, although playing against the wind, the home lot had been hav- ing the best of the game. From now, however, the visitors took up the aggressive, and the ,home defence was hard pressed. Good shots by Rothwell and Sherlock were charged down :by the home defenders, and then Williams was prominent with a good attempt, his final shot hitting the cross-bar and rebounding into play, being cleared by Bramhall. Several other de- termined efforts were repulsed by the home de- fenders, and the visitors failed to score again. Bramhall and Holmes played a splendid defen- sive game for Paddington, and Garnett and Darbyshire were the pick of the forwards; and Winders, Aldridge, F. Eaton, Rothwell, Wil- liams and Plumpton were the most prominent /for the vhitors. Result: Paddington, 1 goal; ,Earlistown Mission, 1 goal. LONGFORD UNITED V. HOLLINS GREEN.

On the Horse and Jockey ground, in the second round of the Cup Competiton, before a small attendance.

Longford: Fearnley; Holden and Mason; Ptevens, Smart and Shelmardine; Finen, Eceleston,_Sherratt, Bennett and Hughes.

Hollins Green: Blundell; Clarke and A. Har- rison; Buckley, F. Harrison and Cleworth ; lohnson, Etchells, Muskett, Gibbons and At- kinson.

Referee, Mr. Aldred, Newton-I(,\Villows

The visitors won the toss and played with a strong wind at their backs. Sherratt kicked off, and after the opening exchanges the visitors ad- vanced into the home half, and following good combination, Muskett passed judiciously to ttchells, and the latter opened the score, after five ,minutes' play. After this good play in mid-field followed, with occasional visits to each goal, and on one occasion the visitors' goal had a narrow escape from being captured. One of the home forwards, when nicely placed, shot hard for goal, and Blundells rushed 'out and saved, the ball rebounding off one of the home players towards the goal, with the cus- todian away. Clarke rushed back and cleared on the, goal line, with a splendid effort. Half- time: Hollins Green, 1 goal; Longford, nil. The home team, with the. advantage of the Wind in the second half, were much smarter on the ball than their • opponents, and after several attacks had been repulsed, they re- turned to the attack with a long passing move- ment, and a long shot from Hughes Was allowed to pass between the uprights. Blundell, ap- pealing for off-side, made no attempt to save, And the home team equalised with a very easy goal. The home team continued to have the hest of the exchanges, and but for the splen- did defence shown by Blundell, Clarke, and A. Harrison, they would have scored several times. 1 The second goal was scored from a scrimmage in the visitors' goal, the goalkeeper asserting that he had put the ball round the post for a corner, but the referee's decision was against him. Sherratt was credited with obtaining the point. The visitors made strenuous efforts to get on equal terms, but owing to the wind and the strong defence of the home team, their efforts were futile, and they were obliged to retire in defence of their own lines. The third goal for the home team was obtained by Stevens with a splendid shot from thirty yards 'range, and was declared to be the best goal ever seen scored on the ground. From now to the close of play each side made efforts to score, but Were not successful to obtain 'further points before the final whistle was sounded. The home players gave .a good all round exhi- tition, and it would be difficult to make any special mention, as they all did their share to obtain the victory. For the visitors, apart from the defenders already mentioned, Muskett, Atkinson and Cleworth were seen to advantage. itesult : Longford United, 3 goals; Hollins Green, 1 goal. ELMWOOD AVENUE CONGREGATIONAL- ISTS v. ST. BARNABAS'.

Owing to the unfit state of the Lovely-lane ground the return fixture between the above clubs was played at Manchester-road.

St. Barna-bail: J. Shaw; Jefferson and Long; O'Neil, P. Challinor and Lee; Sherrington, Ste- xenson, H. Challinor, D. Vernon and Ray. Elmwood: Holt; Mellor and Joe Phoenix; Hazeldine, Finch and Jim Phoenix ;\ T. Ken- drick, W. Phoenix, E. Kendrick, Wells and Littlewood. Referee, Mr. J. Smith. The Congregationalists won the toss and de- cided to play against a stiff breeze. Challinor Get the ball in motion, and Very quickly a visit was paid to each end, this being followed by even mid-field play. Ray receiving a nice pass from Vernon, a determined attack was made on the home goal. Holt cleared in the first.in- stance easily, but when the ball was returned the home goal had a narrow escape. Holt, :when on the ground, just knocked the ball out of reach of several of the Saints' players, and it was successfully got away. After several attempts by the home forwards to get past' the Saints' backs, they were gradually driven back, and H. Challinor shooting just inside the up- right, the Saints scored a very lucky goal. Directly after this success Vernon beat the home backs and sent the ball through a second time nicely out of the reach of Holt. Half- time: St. Barnabas', 2 goals; Elmwood, nil.

On resuming, the Congreptionalists, with the wind behind them, were more dangerous, but Long and Jefferson defended well. The Con- gregationalists failed to improve upon a free kick just outside the penalty area. The home team were having the best of the exchanges, (but were unsteady in front of goal, many shots .going just wide or over the bar. On one occa- sion T. Kendrick grazed the crossbar with a ~good shot, and the visitors made some good efforts to increase their lead, gaining three corner kicks in quick succession. A few min- utes before the close of play the home for- wards missed several good opportunities by erratic shooting. Result: St. Barnabas', •2 coals; Elmwood Congregationalists, nil.

" STARKEY " CUP COMPETITION.

Second Round.

ORFORD WHITE STAR v. FAIRFIELD

UNITED.

On the Fairfield ground, before a good at- tendance of spectators. White Star: •Milkr; Pilling and Evans; Gifford, Denny,and Caldwell; •Barlow, Connor, Meachin, Stubbs and Hankinson.

Fairfield United: Potter; Scott and Bebbing- ton; Gleave, Bunter and Lewis; Long, Wood- house, Millward, Taylor and Watkins. Referee, M. J. Kite.

The White Star captain won the toss, and Millward set the ball rolling. Meachin Ob- tained 'possession and transferred to *Barlow, and the latter tested Potter in the first minute, but the ball was quickly returned. Hunter handled in the penalty area. Stubbs took the kick and failed to convert, Potter saving and clearing well. The visitors played more keenly and immediately returned to the attack, Meachin, Stubbs, and Connor being prominent. They received good support from their half- backs, and it was only by a supreme effort by Potter, Scott and Bebbington during the next five minutes that they kept their lines secure. Hunter was prominent by judicious movements, feeding his forwards well, but they failed to take advantage of the openings offered to them, and Miller was not called upon to handle dur- ing the first half. After fifteen minutes' play Connor sent in a shot which was partially cleared by Potter. Gifford seized the opening and scored. From the centre-kick good com- bination by the White Star forwards resulted in Stubbs scoring the second point. The visit- 'ors were now "all over" the home team, who appeared to have lost heart, and Meachin added the third and fourth goals. Half-time: White Star, 4 goals; Fairfield United, nil. The second half opened in promising style for the home team. Long, securing the ball, ran along the line; and beat Caldwell and Evdns, and, with a long shot, caused Miller much trouble to clear. Millward had a try and missed by inches. 'Then play was trans- ferred to the home end, when Bebbington cleared with a long kick, Pilling returning the leather. Hankinson secured and centred to Meachin, who shot strght at Potter, who cleared. A free kick against Lewis was taken by Caldwell, who scored the fifth goal. Good combination by Taylor, Watkins and Millward transferred to the opposite end, and. a fruitless corner - was for'ced. ,Before the close of play Hankinson added a sixth goal for the visitors. The White Star played well together, with Meachin, Connor, Caldwell and Pilling well to the fore. For the United, Hunter and Mill- ward were the most prominent. Result: Orford White Star, 6 goals; Fairfield United, nil.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDINS.

The meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guard- ians was held on Tuesday afternoon in the Board mord of the Poor Law Institution at Dutton. Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun., presided. INFIRMARY SUBSCRiPTION INCREASED.

The clerk read some correspondence which had taken place between. the Board and the Manchester Infirmary. • The Board, it ap- peared, subscribed six guineas, and this 'en- titled them to send two patients during the year. During the past year they had a third case which they desired to send. It was pointed out by the Infirmary Board that they could not accept a third patient unle,ss the sub- scription was increased. The Guardians had offered to pay three guineas, but that alterna- tive could not be accepted, it being against the rules. The case being a rather urgent one, the Infirmary, however, decided to accept it, in the hope that the Board's subscription would be increased this year. Mr. Norman moved that the subscription be increased to nine guineas. Mr. Pilling seconded.

Mr. Morris asked why the Guardians sent patients to Manchester, when Liverpool was so near. He moved an amendment that they make inquiries at Liverpool, and that the Clerk be instructed to vk rite for terms. • This, however, found no seconder.

The Chairman explained that the Board asked whether they could pay three guineas for every extra patient they sent. They were spending the ratepayers' money, and, they did not :want to throw away even a Sovereign if they could •help it. Mr. Woodward thought Mr. Morris might he under the impression that Liverpool was .the more valuable Place.

Mr. Morris said Liverpool was far more accessible:

The proposition. to increase the subscription -o nine guineas was carried. WHAT TO DO WITH THE BOYS.

The minutes of the Children's Homes Com- mittee being submitted to the meeting for con- firmation, Mr. Harrison protested against send- ing the boys to Messrs. • Pilkington's. He thought it was the wrong way to go to work, whilst there were au Y number of farmers who would be only too glad to take these boys. They were told that by sending them to the farmers they m-itii be doing gctod work for the country by helping to produce food. He moved that the Gutirdians try to get eligible boys situations at farmhouses.

Mr. Antwis seconded the proposition. The Chairman reminded the mover of the resolution that the committee had got a recom- mendation on the books asking members of the committee to try and influence boys to become farm hands. That resolution was passed hi March last, so that it forestalled the present one.

Mr. Harrison said he understood a largo number of their boys had gone to Pilkington's. The Chairman observed that if boys would not go on to the farms, the Board could not make them.

Mr. Haywood, who said he was on the com- mittee, stated that each case was discussed on its merits.

Mr. Harrison then withdrew his amehdment, and the report of the committee was approved.

FINANCE COMMITTEE.

Mr. Woodward submitted the report of the Finance Committee, which showed that the total expenditure for the year had been ,Z,394 14s. 4d, and that the Union was left with a balance in their favour of • .R 2,095 19s. 9d. lie -moved the acceptance of the minutes, and his proposition was carried.

REVISION OF CONTRACTS.

The Clerk reported having received a letter from Messrs. Lawless and Son, of Frodsham, the 'contractors to The Board for bread, stating that owing to tiv'? rise in flour,, over which they had no control; and could not foresee, they were compelled .reluctantly to ask the Guardians if they could meet them in any way. There was similar letter from Messrs. Kydd and Co. of Frodsham, who were the groceries con tractors.

The House Committee had inquired into the matter. and the result was that they recom- mended a revision in the prices as follows: Flour, 38s. per sack; meal, 405.; and cheese, 9d. per lb. In the case of sugar, it was stated, the Guardians had obtained a reduction from 355. to 325. Bd. per bak. In the case of Messrs. Law- less and Son, the price agreed upon by the com- mittee for bread was 6-IS. per 41b. loaf and 30. per 21b. loaf, in place of sd. and 21d. respec- tively. The minutes e ere approved.

A HINT TO CHURCHPEOPLE.

During Lent the children attending the Sun- day school in connection with St. Clement's Mission Church are being asked to contribute towards the education of a boy in Africa, in order that he may be brought up a Christian. This is fhe only time during the whole year when a collection is asked for from the chil- dren. "The Sunday school," says this month's Warrington PariAlt Church Magazine, "is not a charitable institution, although religious in- struction is given free of charge," the writer adding, "and I hope that parents, who do not usually attend public worship, but who send their children to the school, will help in some way to support this branch of the work."

SLACKNESS IN PUBLIC WORSHIP

Vicar of St. Ann's Regrets.

Writing in the current issue of his Paris" Magazine, the Rev. James Dairies, Vicar ol St. Ann's, says :

"Let us continue to intercede at the Throne of Grace for the Divine Blessings and that God may bring success to our forces and the cause for which they are arrayed in battle. We at home must not only help in the efficient equipment in material things as regards our Navy and Army, we must not neglect pleading for Divine help, to whom. alone belong the issues of war. See to it that every opportu- nity for intercession in private and in public worship is fully used. We cannot but greatly regret the slackness of very many in their attendance on Sunday and Thursday at our public worship, for prayer, thanksgiving and praise. Wet counsel each to see that there is no neglecting of the assembling together in God'S _ _

HO-use and no slacking of -private and family supplication for God's help for our forces and those of our Allies."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE CHURCH INTERCESSION SERVICE.

On. Thursday evening of last week the monthly service of intercession in connection with the Warrington Free Church Council was held. Since the commencement of the war these intercession services have been held monthly on the second Tuesday evening of the month in some Free Church in the centre of the town. On Thursday the service was held in the Bewsey-road Wesleyan 'Church, when the Rev. W. T. Derry, the superintendent minis- ter. presided over a fair attendance, and the address was given by the Rev. A. J. Chessnm, who emphasised the need for all Free Chuirch- men to realise the Real Presence of the Christ —the Christ who lived in every-day life awl worked with every-day men. In this time of terror, when affairs were out of joint, they needed, he said, to realise that "The Son of Man" was amongst them. The Revs. W. T. Derry and 11. Ogden and Mr. J. Upson and others joined in the service.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0251.txt | included=true | included =====
TWENTY-TWO YEARS OLD.

‘N ye] if fe Brotherhood Keep An Interesting Anniversary.

The members of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Brother- hood celebrated the twenty-second anniversary of the inauguration of their society on Sunday afternoon, when a largely-attended open meet- ing was held in the Wycliffe Congregational Church, and a special address was given by Mr. Robert Lewis, of Ashton-in-Makerfield. The anniversary service was of a bright and hearty character and was presided over by the Pastor (the Rev. T. T. James). The church choir were in attendance and contributed three concerted items in fine style—" The Watch- word," "The Radiant Morn," and "The Com- rades' Song of Hope."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
A Time for Review and for Hope.

Mr. S. W. Jeffery having read the lesson, the Rev. T. T. James said that that service was an occasion for gratitude and for ambition- On such an occasion they had not only to cast their minds back in review of the past, but also look forward to the future. He believed that there were great opportunities ahead of a society such as theirs, and it was essential that they should go forward with the feeling that there was still work to be done, and that it was their duty to do their share of it. He trusted that when their anniversary again came round they would be able to congratu- late themselves upon yet another happy and successful year. They were now living in anxious times, and those brief Sunday after- noon services helped them to go forward with new hearts and fresh courage. Many of their members had gone from amongst them to play their parts in the great battles now being waged on the Continent of Europe, and they hoped and prayed that the day might soon come when, the war over, those members might once more gather with them at those Sunday afterntion meetings—join with them in a heart- felt service of thanksgiving.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
The Value of Endurance.

Mr. Lewis then addressed the meeting on the subject of "Endurance," and said that he believed in Christians being not merely pas- sive, but, also, aggressive, when occasion war- ranted. He wanted them to he aggressive in the matter of organising that which would prove of „value for the construction of genuine character. Endurance was the index of Chris- tian development; it was a necessity in the equipment of every good Christian, because of the temptations which beset his path. Very often, the speaker said, the greatest endurance had been found amongst those who had had the. greatest physical suffering to face; indeed, some of the greatest benefactors to the race had been men who had had more than their share of physical suffering; as, for instance 4 John Milton, Dr. Johnson, Henry Fawcett (the blind Postmaster-General), and John Bunyan.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0254.txt | included=true | included =====

A good deal has happened in that period. And I quite understand how recent and pres-mt national events have acted prejudicially upon the P.S.A. movement—not only in Wycliffe, Warrington, but also throughout the kingdom. Our P.S.A.. however, must pluck up heart and cherish faith and abound in prayer, in earnest endeavour to enlist the men of Warrington, large numbers of whom may yet be secured. "I know full well how depressing it must be to see the lessened attendances which I understand to be now so frequent. But I would most strongly stimulate the Brotherhood to overcome that present discouragement by a resolute effort to gather in, and retain new adherents. Warrington has taken a noble

===== 0002601_19150313_art0255.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTH SEA BATTLE.

Local Man on the " Lion "

Describes the Action.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0256.txt | included=true | included =====
STRANGE SUNDAY SCENES.

By HAROLD MAYBURY:

(Author of.'" The Tulip Farms of Haarlem.") Mr. Harold Maybury gives below the result of an interview he recently had with a local man who was on H.M.S. Lion in the North Sea action, when the German " Bluecher" was sunk and other large enemy vessels were seriously damaged.

It is the dawn of another day. All around, stretching as far as the eye could see, the boundless ocean, a deep, glorious blue, the crests of the waves gently tipped with white and in the midst the pulsing, throbbing leviathan, H.M.S. Lipn, plodding her way through the North Sea. Following in the wake of our boat was a flock of seagulls, their shrill cries resounding through the clear morning air, seemingly asking for scraps of food from those on deck. For weeks nothing had happened to end the dull, monotonous routine of our naval life, and on this glorious Sabbath morn of January 24th we were expecting the same end- less watching and waiting for something to happen. How little any of us thought of what the coming hours held in store' I was just finishing one of xny duties when I observed a speck so small it was hardly notice- able on the horizon, then another, until I counted four. No sooner had I noticed them than the order was given to "Prepare for action." They were German warships: The hour was eight o'clock. My comrades and I perceptibly felt the speed of our boat increase as she churned her way through the calm sea. Not three minutes had elapsed before'everypne was at his post, full of elation at the prospect of revenging Scarborough and Whitby.. "Are they coming to meet us?" I whispered to my friend close by, but he did not answer. From the funnels of our boat the great black clouds of smoke belched forth, adding an addi- tional colour to the scene.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0257.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FIGHT BEGINS.

Very soon I realised that the enemy did not intend to meet us openly, aild my spirits fell as I thought that, after all, they might escape. So the chase went on for some little time, maybe an hour, but to me it-seemed ages. At last, from one of the big guns on our "A" turret the first shot was fired, the Messenger of Death flying through the air with lightning- like velocity. It missed, being too high, but it was a near thing. The target was fifteen miles away, and dash- ing through the seas at the speed of a train, trying to escape. Our battle squadron were advancing in line formation, the Indomitable being obscured from view by the dense black smoke pouring from our funnels. Again we fired, this time with more success. We had -drawn nearer to our prey and were manceuvring, as I saw from the signals. By this time the German cruisers had opened fire. All around the shells were falling thick and fast, the sea in front rising to a great height where the 'German shells struck. The seagulls had disappeared, evidently frightened by the firing. Nearer and nearer we drew, slowly and persistently. The stokers below went mad, intoxicated in the heat of battle, getting every ounce Out of our beloved vessel.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0258.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WOUNDED LION.

So the battle went on grim, stern and relent- less. Away in the distance I could see the Germans in difficulties, the Bluecher heeling over badly from the brilliant marksmanship of the gunners of our squadron. Already I saw vision of the "coup-de-grace" for the whole of the enemy, but it was not to be. A well-aimed shot from one of the retreating vessels crashed into the side of H.D.E.S. Lion, and she shud- dered from •stem to stern. Instantly our speed decreased to ascertain the damage. Water poured into us and the pumps were brought into operation. It was not serious, but enough to put us out of continuing in the glorious work. There was no confusion, just regret, at the ill-luck. As I stood on board, away in the distance, very faint, could be seen Heligoland, so near and yet so far. It was nearing the noonday hour. Across the blue expanse of water three flotillas of our destroyers hastened to our rescue. Our engines had stopped, and as I stood at my post a man passed me. It was Vice-Admiral Sir David Beatty. He spoke no words. Round his lips a ghost of a smile lingered; that was all. He stepped on board one of the destroyers and soon was lost to sight. - 'British Courage .had won the day. For 230 miles the Indomitable towed us back to 'dear old England, where we landed at 12 noon on Tuesday, ..the 26th. Sometimes now, as I think of that Sabbath Day, which will be added to history, I Seem to see the seagulls in the early dawn, their shrill cries ringing out as our vessel ploughed through the waves (and I wonder whether or not in that language of their own they had not a message for us of what the day held in store. 'Tis passing strange that when we sighted the Germans they disappeared. The day will ever be remembered. by me, as it is the most eventful one I have ever passed— yet who knows? The waves and the seagulls are calling!

I go btiOk to my ship next week; "good- bye."

===== 0002601_19150313_art0259.txt | included=true | included =====
STOP THAT RHEUMATISM.

"Stop it now, at once! There is no need for you to suffer the pangs and agonies of Rheuma- tism any longer. Every day, every hour almost, that you continue to suffer is self-inflicted tor- ture." This emphatic statement is made by Dr. Geo. E. Flood, whose successful labours to find a suitable and effective remedy for Rheu- matism, and not merely a means of temporary relief, have made his name famous all the world over.

For the benefit of our readers, we append the prescription which he recommends. It is : Syrup of Ginger Liquid Barkola Compound Syrup of Rhubarb Tincture of Gentian

Dose: A teaspoonful after meals and at bed- time. We have the assurance of the Doctor that this prescription (which can be dispensed by any chemist) can be taken with perfect safety by the most delicate man or woman. The first dose usually brings relief, aid if persisted in for a very short time will result in a perfect cure of the ailment.

The Local Leather Trade.

"The trade during last week," says the War- rington correspondent of "The Leather Trades 'Review," "was on much the usual lines. There was a very strong demand for full deliveries against contracts, but very little interest was shown in further business. It looks as thaugil the whole trade is being held up pending an announcement from the War Office as to the alterations they will make in the next con- tract. That some change is likely seems to be generally expected. While future events are thus uncertain, matters at present are decid- edly brisk, and tanners are hard pressed for deliveries. The output is very heavy, and is apparently cut up as fast as delivered." ;

ANOTHER PENNY IN THE 1.

War Again Touches the Rate- payers' Pockets.

WHY THE GUARDIANS FIND A RISE NECESSARY.

Mr2A. J. Jolley presided over the fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guar- dians, which was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0262.txt | included=true | included =====
ALL-ROUND PATRIOTISM.

The Rev. H Ogden and the Clyde Dispute. STRIKERS AND PROFIT-MAKERS.

At Golborne-street Baptist Church last Sun- day evening the Rev. H. Ogden preached upon the subject, "The War and the Clyde," taking for his text Nehemiah iv. 18: "For every one had his sword girded by his side, and so builded." The preacher alluded to the origin of Spurgeon's phrase, "The sword and the trowel," as an evidence of the great preacher's knack of phrasing. The preacher went on to say that the servant of God, Nehemiah, was faced with the constructive work of building the walls, and defending them as they were built, hence the warriors wrought half their time at building and half their time at fight- ing. Building as a work of peace was as neces- sary as the work of war. Arts of Peace Help Men of War.

The utterance of a great and responsible statesman emphasised how necessary were the engines of peace to the men of war and blood and red ruin. The action of the men on the Clyde had stirred all Downing-street. The flame on the banks of the Scotch river had needed the attention of no ordinary fireman. All Downing-street had turned out: The im- portance of the munitions of war had just cal- phasised again how helpless were directors of war without the directors of peace. The War Office was helpless before the actions of the rank and file of the armies of peace. War was an incident. Peace was the normal life. Yet the incident would unbalance the normal life for a century.. The rank and file of all nations mould surely put Foreign Ministers and diplo- mats in their right focus at the end of this mar.

' The Diminished Power of the Sovereign.

The action of the Clyde men was unpatii?tie, some patriots said. They ought not to have struck work. They should have waited for Government action. Governments act, as a rule, when compelled. Free Church Councils had passed resolutions which had been for- warded to the Government. They had been answered by a twentieth Under Secretary. Nothing had been done. Governments under- stand drastic action. One hoped now that the men would receive justice. It must be borne in mind that £1 in July last was worth now about 14s. 6d. -in Glasgow. The men had toiled through long hours of overtime. Flesh and blood would give way. Why was the Govern- ment so tardy in its action?

Circumstances That Alter Cases—But Should They?

But patriotism is a wide term. A workman on a Government contract who went on strike just now was unpatriotic. A shipper, a coal- owner, a huge wheat factor reaped unparalleled profits and no one interfered. Whoever robbed a child of a slice of bread was a traitor. Trusts rose the prices. Then trusts could pull them down. But a trust had no heart! It had no bowels of mercy, and whilst the Clyde work- men had, been warned of very severe conse- quences, shippers, coalowners and wheat fac- tors had grown rich on the starved grates and empty stomachs of the poor. A certain Mr. Meyer had made five times the salary of a Cabinet Minister, because the nation needed timber. Let patriotism have a universal appli- cation. The Clyde men might have been thoughtless in their action, but what of the men who, of forethought and wilful intent, had robbed the poor dnd struck hard bargains with a nation in trouble.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0263.txt | included=true | included =====
The Ravages of Drink—" As Usual."

Meanwhile the ravages of drink pursued their awful course. Last week, the preacher said, he saw in Winwick-road a soldier in the King's uniform helplessly drunk. Half a dozen kind- hearted women were endeavouring to carry— not lead—him from public shame. In this town the temperance people were fatally im- potent. The Chancellor of the Exchequer had threatened the Trade. They would await with interest to see if he was as strong as his threats. The drink curse still remained upon them. They were told that the public-house was worth an army corps to the enemy. Our men, civil and military,. were demoralised by its effects, and yet the curse was still allowed to remain upan us. The Clyde men must be hustled back to work; "The Trade" still continued. "Business as usual." Every barrel of beer was a bomb in the life of the nation, wreck- ing useful men, destroying women, and blast- ing the fair hopes of the child. Let them have patriotism all round. Hustle the Clyde men back if they would, but let the Government hustle also the greedy trust, and the capitalist, and throttle a trade which still cursed the nation.

• , • Metereplogical. The mean temperature locally daring the week ended March 6th was 45 degrees, or 9 degrees higher than in the previous week. The warmest day recently was the 3rd inst., and the coldest the 28th ult., the ,temperatures on those days being 55 and 34 respbctively. The rainfall last week was .65 inches, and the general direction of the wind was south- westerly.

CATHOLIC SCHOOLS FOR CATHOLIC CHILDREN.

It will be recalled that at -their previous meeting the Guardians decided to send the Roman Catholic children at present in the Pad- gate Cottage Homes to special Catholic schools, certified by the Local Government Board as being suitable for the purpose of training Poor. Law children.

The Catholic organisation responsible for the governing of the schools in question now wrote that. they were prepared to take the boys over eight years of age in St-Vincent's Home, Ful- wood, Preston; and boys under eight and girls under 16 in the Greenfield House School, Bil- linge. The maximum charge per week in the certified 'schools was is., but they did not pro- pose to make any change in the amount pre- viously paid by the Board for the Maintenance of the children, viz., 6s. a week.

_ Mr. Brooks moved that the terms be ac- cepted, and this having been carried, the Clerk was instructed to take the necessary steps to get the, children transferred, and to make Special arrangements with regard to ." in .and cut," cases. . The Padgate. Cottage Homes Committee re- commended that the Clerk be instructed to communicate ,with the Warrington Education Committee and the authorities of the Church of England Schools, Padgate, with a view to making arrangements for the children, other than those to be transferred to Roman Catho- lic certified schools, to. attend the day schools at Oakwood-avenue and Padgate. The recommendation was endorsed by the Board.

THE PRICE OF TEA.

Writing with regard to the Board's adver- tisement for tea tenders, a firm of tea mer- chants reminded them that. it was no longer possible to quote anything like the old prices, viz., is. 4d. to is. 6d. per lb. The cheapest Ceylon tea was now being quoted at 101 d. per lb., without the duty-tax, which brought it up to Is. 6-id. a lb. Consequently they did not think the Board would be able to buy drink- able tea at less than is. &I. and is. 9d. ' Mr. Mather: And we can't turn to ale, be- cause • there's an extra halfpenny a gill on that! (Laughter.)

TURNING THE ASYLUM INTO A RED CROSS HOSPITAL.

The Guardians of the Township of Manches- ter wrote inviting the Warrington Board to join in a protest against the proposal to turn the Winwick County Asylum into a hospital for sick and wounded soldiers, opining that accommodation of the kind required could be found elsewhere without encroaching on the housing accommodation so much required, and so suitable for cases of acute insanity, and the deprivation of which would inflict hardship upon the insane. The Chairman said that at their last meet- ing the Board decided to take no action in the matter.

Mr. E. L. Bent moved that the Warrington Board forward a resolution, on the lines sug- gested in the letter from Manchester, to the Lancashire County Asylums Board. He did not agree with the proposal to move the luna- tics, and reminded the Guardians that they were already arranging ,to accommodate about 160 wo tallied soldiers in their new buildings, If those responsible only looked around he was sure that they could find suitable build- ings for their purpose, buildings that could soon be adapted for the accommodation of wounded soldiers.

Mr. Bent's proposal found no seconder. Later on in the meeting a letter was read from the Medical Superintendent at the Win- wick Asylum stating that as the asylum is to be taken over by the war, no new patients would be admitted until further notice.

Mr. Mather: We shall have to begin leav- ing them at home. Mr. Knowles: They will have to give over going insane. (Laughter.)

===== 0002601_19150313_art0268.txt | included=true | included =====
PLEASANTLY SURPRISED.

Mr. Shaw called attention to the fact that the Local Government Board only proposed to allow a term of ten years for the repayment of the money tp be borrowed for the furnish- ing of the new block of buildings, and thought the Board should go in for a longer period, else it meant that the present generation would be paying for the whole thing, and that posterity, whilst having the use pf the furni- ture, would be paying nothing. The Chairman said that he would have thought that the Government would not have allowed more than five, or at most seven, years for the repayment of the money. The Clerk: I am pleasantly surprised that they are willing to give us so long a period as ten years. The item for furnishing includes .RBOO for bedding and that will certainly not last ten years.

THE POOR TAW BUDGET And An Explanation.

The Finance Committee reported that they had considered the Clerk's estimate of expen- diture for the half-year ending September 30th next. They recommended that calls be made upon the Overseers for the several townships within the Union for the sum of £19,710. GUARDIANS' EXPENSES £1,600 UP.

The Chairman, in proposing the adoption of the estimates', said that the estimated expen- diture, as compared with the actual expendi- ture for the current year, showed an increase of approximately A3OO, but as the actual ex- penditure included 11,333 10s. for land for the proposed new offices, paid for out of revenue, the increase in the estimated expenditure was really about 416,000 more than the normal. That was to say, there vas an increase of about 8 per cent. in the total, ,and that in- crease was almost entirely due to 'the increased cost of food and materials generally and to the extra asylum charges, which, of course, were also due to the same cause as the in- creases in the prices of food-stuffs, viz., the war. He would like•to take that opportunity. of emphasising a point he had mentioned be- fore. For several half-years they had worked to some extent upon balances in hand; in fact, they had carried that sort of thing so far that it was impracticable now' to carry it any fur- ther, for the balances were now dangerously , Ipw.

===== 0002601_19150313_art0270.txt | included=true | included =====
SMALL RISE, DESPITE k ABNORMAL TIMES.

The Vice-chairman (Mr. W. Furness), in seconding, said that the Board might congratu- late themselves and the ratepayers that, not- withstanding the abnormal times, they had not found it necessary tO call for more than an extra penny in the C. He desired to draw the attention of the Board to a statement made by the Chairman of the Finance Com- mittee of the Town Council at last week's meeting of that body. In his Budget speech that gentleman said that the Overseers would have to meet an increased call from the Guar- dians the levying of a poor rate of ss. in the an increase of 4d. in the X. That statement going broadcast, as it had done, had misled the pro- perty owners and ratepayers. In justice to themselve4 and to put them clear in the eyes of the public, be had prepared a statement showing exactly how that sc. was being spent.

Town Council Spend Most of the Poor Rate. It was not being spent by the Board, but primarily by the Town Council. "A rate of ss. in the X is being levied by the Overseers of the Poor," said Mr. Furness, "and it will

OU SU

Ii

be as well to inquire and see how the rate is spent. The cost of the police and of the ad- ministration of justice accounts for 5.20 d. in the and other items are: Museum and Library, 1.50 d.; Cemetery, .33d.; Town Hall, 1.03 d.; baths, 1.62 d.; education, 24.37 d.; bridges, 1.22 d.; asylums, 1.22 d.; financial adjustment With county, 1.06 d.; administration, 3.77 d.; sundries less .receipts, .87d.; a total of 42.88 d. out of 60d. That means that the Warrington Corporation spend 3s. 61d. out of the 55., and that we get is. sd. and the other farthing goes for Overseers' expenses. As far as the Guar- dians were concerned, they ran their work on most economical lines,- and they ought to have credit for doing 59." GUARDIANS GET Is. sd. OUT OF ss. RATE.

The Clerk said that thousands of people in Warrington did not pay their rates direct, and. consequently, when they heard that the Poor Rate was 55., they. Would naturally assume that that ss. was for the upkeep of the poor, 'where- as the truth was that the amount the Guar- dians received out of the ss. was Is. sd. He was glad the Matter had been mentioned. and made clear, for two or three persons had men- tioned the matter to him during the last few days and had wondered what the Guardians' did with the money. The Chairman: The Guardians would only , like to have hold of it. Still, I cannot help thinking that .the average ratepayer will not bother very much about differentiating, 1-t ecm— the two bodies that spend their money, but will feel inclined to say "a • plague on both your houses.'.! (Laughter.) The proposition was carried. NEW RELIEVING OFFICER APPOINTED. 1,,

The House Committee reported that they had considered 56 applications for the position of Relieving Officer for the No. 4 District, and had selected the following to appear before the Board: Messrs. A. E. Baker, Joseph Hill, Mark Pharaoh, Frank Plumpton and William Rut, ter, all of Warrington, and Mr.- Herbert Pen. nington, of Haydock. • After two rounds of voting in camera it was announced that Mr. Pennington had. been chosen for the position, securing 15 'votes, Mr. Baker being the runner-up with 6 votes. WHAT ANALYSIS SHOWED. It was reported that at a meeting of the Padgate Cottage Homes Committee, Mr. F. G. Ruddock's report upon three samples of water which he had analysed—brook water, well water, and Warrington Corporation water— was submitted. The committee recommended that no brook water be used at the Cottage Homes in future, and that the report be re- ferred to the special committee appointed to deal with the alterations at the Homes. The report was adopted.

IMPROVED ACCOMMODATION WANTED.

The House Committee reported having con- sidered the present unsatisfactory condition of the accommodation for the lunatics and im- beciles and recommended that the Chairman. of the' Committee (Mr. Brooks), together with the Mastei., be authorised to alter and .imprgve upon the present arrangements. It was fur- ther, decided that the services- of three tem- porary attendants be obtained (two males, in- cluding one with male-nurse qualifications, and one female), such temporary officers to reside: in the institution, if possible.

RUNCORN UNION. CONTRACTS FOR PROVISIONS AND SUPPLIES For the Half-year ending September 30th, 1915. The Guardians of the Poor of the Runcorn Union are desirous of receiving TENDERS from parties' willing to Contract for the supply of: Good- Beef (to consist of 'vein pieces intact and Flat Ribs with bone, one round weekly not to exceed 251 b., and ,451 b. per week of the -Best Butcher's Meat, including Veal, Lamb and Pork (in seatton) for the Officers), Sides of Mutton (all meat to be English killed), Bread, Groceries, Drapery, Clothing, Boots, Boot Repairing, Earthenware, Drysaltery, Brushes and Mats, Wines, Spirits, Coal, Slack, Coke, Deal Coffins, Horse Hire (for Funerals and Carting), Milk (for 12 months), Tobacco and Snuff, Oils and Colours. TENDERS are also invited from Tradesmen for the services per hour of Journeymen and Apprentices of the following trades: Plumber, Joiner, Whitesmith, and Bricklayer. Parties tendering for the supply of the above- mentioned Goods will be required to deliver the same at the Workhouse and Children's Home, both situate in Dutton, free of cost to the Guardians, how and when required. Tenders tobe accompanied with samples. (ex- cept Drapery Goods and Clothing) as far as prac- ticable. The Guardians do not bind themselves to accept the lowest or any Tender. The figures published in the report shoic that an ade- quate gross profit was made sufficient to cover these charges aad leave a surplus. The difference between food cost and value of eggs produced awas 12s. per bird for the 'whole !look.
